Product

QA test plan

Answers OQ-13 — who runs the acceptance tests and how often — and enumerates every test scenario that follows from it: six hundred and eighty-eight cases, positive and negative, across the four deploy automations, the product seed script and the two Go test packages that actually run today — forty-four of them retired with the CLI execution layer in v0.29.0 and struck rather than deleted — all thirty-six of the gateway's forward specifications now running, plus the two business acceptance tests BRD §4.1 specifies and nothing has yet executed — the third, the fold test, runs as of v0.11.0 and has moved into the register proper. Since D-039 this register is also the project's definition of success: GAOS is done when every case here passes, positive and negative alike, and no numeric outcome target stands beside it.

How to read this register

Every row is a TC-nnn. Kind is Positive (the invariant holds, or the action succeeds) or Negative (the invariant is violated, or the action is refused) — both are listed for almost every scenario, because a check that never fails is unverified, not passing. Status is Implemented (the automation exists and this page describes its real, current behaviour, re-read from deploy/check-stacks.sh and deploy/rebuild-restart.sh at the time this page was written), Not implemented (a forward specification for a test the design commits to but nothing in the repository can run yet), or Retired (a case whose premise a later rule overturned — struck, never deleted and never renumbered, with the reason and the replacement on the row, because an ID that silently disappears is an ID somebody will reuse).

Who runs these tests, and how often

GAOS has no CI configuration anywhere in the repository — these automations only run when a human or agent invokes them by hand. BRD §5.1 names four internal roles — product owner, platform operator, fleet operator, record author — and notes they are currently one person. That person is the runner for everything below, whether they invoke deploy/rebuild-restart.sh directly or through the gaos-deploy subagent, which exists precisely so the same deterministic script runs the same way every time instead of a free-handed docker compose command drifting between invocations.

AutomationRunnerTrigger / cadenceOn failure
check-stacks.shProduct owner, via rebuild-restart.sh or standaloneAutomatically, as the first step of every deploy and restart — both tiers. Never runs for statusRefuses the action outright, exit 1, before Docker is touched. The operator fixes the offending compose or Traefik file and reruns
go test ./... (dashboard, pustaka) + pustaka check ./docsSameAutomatically, before every deploy action only — restart has no test gate at allRefuses the build, exit 1. The operator fixes the code or docs and reruns
Post-deploy verification (bind, healthz, routes, base-href, and with --full-verify: X-Forwarded-For, public HTTPS)SameAutomatically, after every deploy and restartEach check reports independently; the run exits 1 on any failure, but nothing rolls back automatically today — the nonzero exit is the operator's signal to investigate
rebuild-restart.sh statusSameOn demand, whenever the operator wants a read of current stateNo failure mode by design — see TC-017
deploy/schema/scratch.sh + go test -run ConformanceProduct owner, on demandNever automatically: the ArcadeDB half of the store conformance suite skips without GAOS_ARCADE_TEST_URL, so the deploy gate stays green on a machine with no database. Run it by hand when the store or the schema changesNothing is deployed, because nothing was — the throwaway container is --rm with no volume. The operator reads the failure and reruns
deploy/acceptance/kill-mid-task.shProduct owner, before every release that touches the runner, the lease or recoveryNever automatically, and never against dev or prod: it needs a scratch ArcadeDB, it sends a real SIGKILL, and recovery writes to the graph. It also cannot run against GAOS_STORE=memory, which loses the task records there would be anything to reconcileNothing is deployed. This is build-order step 5 and a failure is a release blocker, not a warning — a runner whose work does not survive a restart is a runner that loses paid model calls
deploy/schema/apply.shProduct owner, on demandOnly for a scratch database from a workstation. Dev and prod get the schema from the dashboard binary's embedded copy on every boot, which is idempotentExit 1 with the offending statement and the server's own detail. A partial apply is safe to rerun
Unsatisfiable-task testProduct owner, once domain E existsProposed: before every releasePer §7.5: looping or failing silently means the guard-limit implementation is not finished — release is blocked
Fold testProduct owner, once domain H existsProposed: periodic (weekly) — the design itself calls this test "periodic"Divergence is a release blocker, in the design's own words: "divergence proves the event model is incomplete"
Second-run testProduct ownerOn demand; at minimum once, to seed the numeric baseline that has never existed — D-039 leaves outcome targets unset until this run happensA viability signal, not a gate: per §1.4, no improvement means "the extraction step is decorative"

This is the answer to OQ-13, logged as D-025.

All six hundred and eighty-eight cases in one filterable table. Filter by automation, kind, or any word in a scenario — go test narrows it to the unit tests, check-stacks to the deploy pre-flight.

Test scenarios — implemented automations

Everything below runs today, in deploy/check-stacks.sh, deploy/rebuild-restart.sh, deploy/schema/scratch.sh and the two Go test packages, dashboard/ and pustaka/. Every expected message is quoted from the script or the test that produces it, not paraphrased.

check-stacks.sh — tier database isolation (TC-001, TC-002)

The script's own header names this the expensive check: a dev stack pointed at the production database starts cleanly, passes every smoke test, and is invisible at runtime. Since 2026-11-02 both tiers use one ArcadeDB server, the shared one on clinodb, reached over WireGuard at http://10.10.0.5:2480 through deploy/clinodb/compose.arcade-bridge.yml; the boundary is now the database and its user — gaos_dev read by user gaos_dev, gaos by gaos_prod, each refused (403) on the other's, which was measured when the users were created. Positive (TC-001): the files as committed print ok dev uses the gaos_dev database, ok dev does not use the PROD database, ok prod uses the gaos database (overridable per instance) (prod names it as "${GAOS_ARCADE_DB:-gaos}", so a second instance can set its own database), ok prod does not use the DEV database, ok dev reaches the database over WireGuard and ok prod reaches the database over WireGuard (prod names the URL as "${GAOS_ARCADE_URL:-http://10.10.0.5:2480}", so a second instance can point at its own ArcadeDB). Negative (TC-002): set dev's GAOS_ARCADE_DB to "gaos" — FAIL dev uses the gaos_dev database and FAIL dev does not use the PROD database, and the run exits 1.

check-stacks.sh — tunnel-only publish binding (TC-003, TC-004)

Without the 10.10.0.2 prefix a ports: mapping binds every interface the host has, including the LAN — and nothing at runtime says so. Positive (TC-003): dev publishes "10.10.0.2:19301:80", prod publishes "${GAOS_TUNNEL_IP:-10.10.0.2}:18301:80" (the default is vm-rnd-ardi; a second instance sets GAOS_TUNNEL_IP to its own wg address), each file has exactly one ports: mapping. Negative (TC-004): drop the address prefix ("19301:80") or add a second mapping — FAIL unexpected bind or FAIL n ports: mappings, want 1.

check-stacks.sh — no neighbour's network (TC-005, TC-006)

Least privilege as topology. Until 2026-11-02 the dashboard alone joined the shared infra network to resolve ArcadeDB by name; with the database an address on the tunnel, no service needs a neighbour's network at all. Positive (TC-005): ok compose.dev.yaml: no service on a shared infra network, and the same for prod. Negative (TC-006): put a service back on [internal, infra] — FAIL compose.dev.yaml: 4 line(s) join a shared infra network, want none (the count is every matching line in the file).

check-stacks.sh — distinct project identity (TC-007, TC-008)

Positive (TC-007): name: gaos-dev / 172.30.13.0/24 and name: gaos-prod / 172.30.12.0/24, neither reusing 172.30.10.0/24 or 172.30.11.0/24 (the neighbouring product's range). Negative (TC-008): set prod's subnet to 172.30.10.0/24 — FAIL prod does not reuse a neighbour's subnet.

check-stacks.sh — cross-tier parity (TC-009, TC-010)

The Secure cookie flag is env-driven, never scheme-derived — both tiers sit behind real TLS at the edge and see plain HTTP inside the tunnel, per D-023. Positive (TC-009): both files carry GAOS_COOKIE_SECURE: "1". The database name used to be asserted equal here too; since both tiers share one server it must differ, and that moved to TC-001. Negative (TC-010): turn dev's flag off "because it's dev" — FAIL dev keeps Secure cookies on — exactly the bug D-023 exists to prevent.

check-stacks.sh — no Docker socket on the proxy (TC-011, TC-012)

Positive (TC-011): no compose file mounts /var/run/docker.sock, and deploy/stacks/traefik.yml uses the file provider, not docker:. Negative (TC-012): mount the socket into a Traefik service — FAIL dev traefik has no Docker socket.

check-stacks.sh — no committed secret (TC-013, TC-014)

Positive (TC-013): no deploy/stacks/*.env file other than *.example is tracked by git, and neither compose file bakes a literal PASSWORD: "…". Negative (TC-014): git add a real docs.env — git ls-files --error-unmatch succeeds, so the check prints FAIL deploy/stacks/docs.env is tracked by git. Since 2026-11-02 the same loop covers the gateway's two secret files, deploy/stacks/*.aim-* (the ai-stack login and ai-manager's admin key, mounted read-only into the dev dashboard): ok dev.aim-chat.json is untracked, or FAIL deploy/stacks/dev.aim-chat.json is tracked by git. Since v0.37.0 (D-072) it covers the machine API's three token files, deploy/stacks/*.api-* (prod.api-admin, prod.api-qa, prod.api-qa-out): ok prod.api-qa is untracked, or FAIL deploy/stacks/prod.api-qa is tracked by git, and FAIL deploy/stacks/prod.api-qa is not a regular file (a missing bind-mount source is created by Docker as a root-owned directory) when one is a directory.

check-stacks.sh — trailing-slash redirects (TC-015, TC-016)

Without these the <base href> resolves one level up and every asset 404s, while the document itself still answers 200 — silent, browser-side, invisible to any status-code check. Positive (TC-015): deploy/traefik/dynamic.yml defines the /app and /docs redirect rules, and the landing catch-all has priority: 1. Negative (TC-016): remove the /docs$ rule — FAIL the /docs trailing-slash redirect is present.

rebuild-restart.sh — the status action (TC-017)

Positive: rebuild-restart.sh dev status prints docker compose ps plus one healthz probe over SSH through the tunnel, mutates nothing, and always exits 0 — including when the SSH probe itself fails or times out, since status returns before the pre-flight section runs at all and has no verification contract, only a report. There is no negative case in the usual sense: a broken tunnel shows up as raw error text in the output, not a nonzero exit.

rebuild-restart.sh — argument validation (TC-018, TC-019, TC-020)

All three are refused before any git, Docker or SSH call. TC-018: rebuild-restart.sh dev (no action) or rebuild-restart.sh deploy (no environment) prints usage() and exits 2. TC-019: rebuild-restart.sh dev deploy bogus-service prints unknown service 'bogus-service' -- valid: traefik landing dashboard docs, exits 2. TC-020: rebuild-restart.sh dev prod deploy prints environment given twice, exits 2 — the same path catches an action given twice.

rebuild-restart.sh — the check-stacks.sh gate (TC-021, TC-022)

Applies identically to deploy and restart, both tiers. Positive (TC-021): check-stacks.sh exits 0 → ok "check-stacks.sh passed", the action proceeds. Negative (TC-022): any TC-002/TC-004/etc. mutation is present → bad "check-stacks.sh failed -- refusing to $ACTION", exit 1, before Docker is touched — dev and prod are blocked equally.

rebuild-restart.sh — the prod dirty-tree gate (TC-023, TC-024, TC-025)

Prod builds from the working tree directly — there is no separate worktree yet (see deploy/STACKS.md, "Known gaps"). Negative (TC-023): git status --porcelain is non-empty on a prod action without --allow-dirty → refuses, prints the dirty file list, exit 1. Positive (TC-024): same dirty tree with --allow-dirty → proceeds, echoing "working tree is dirty -- proceeding, --allow-dirty was passed" and the file list. Positive, informational (TC-025): dev with a dirty tree — the dirty check is wrapped in if [ "$ENVIRON" = prod ], so dev is structurally exempt; there is no violation to demonstrate.

rebuild-restart.sh — the deploy test gate (TC-026, TC-027, TC-028, TC-029)

Runs only inside if [ "$ACTION" = deploy ] — restart has no test gate at all. Three checks run in order and stop at the first failure: TC-026: dashboard go test ./... fails → bad "dashboard tests failed", exit 1, pustaka's tests never run. TC-027: pustaka go test ./... fails → bad "pustaka tests failed", exit 1. TC-028: pustaka check ./docs fails (a broken internal link, a missing page-meta field, a stale registry entry) → bad "pustaka docs check failed", exit 1. TC-029, positive: all three pass → proceeds to docker compose build.

check-stacks.sh — no vendored asset ships without its licence (TC-150, TC-151)

Apache-2.0 redistribution means keeping the licence text of what you bundle, and deploy/Dockerfile.pustaka copies docs/ wholesale into the published image. So a font added without its OFL text ships in every image built afterwards, and nothing anywhere says so — the failure is a legal one with no runtime symptom at all. This is the only place that would notice. Positive (TC-150): one check per font family rather than per weight, because the licence covers the family — atkinson-hyperlegible has its licence text, bricolage-grotesque has its licence text, jetbrains-mono has its licence text — then the charting library and what travels with it: echarts has its licence text, echarts has its NOTICE (Apache 2.0 section 4(d) requires the NOTICE separately from the licence), and the d3 subcomponent has its licence text, because ECharts embeds BSD 3-Clause d3 code and its own LICENSE points at a text it does not itself contain. Negative (TC-151): a family whose text is missing or empty fails with <family> has no LICENSE-<family>.txt; the library's three fail with echarts has no LICENSE-echarts.txt, echarts has no NOTICE-echarts.txt and echarts cites LICENSE-d3 and none is vendored. Any of them makes the script exit 1, which stops the deploy at pre-flight.

check-stacks.sh — the vendored sisflow board ships with its grant (TC-310)

T-165. The sisflow board is vendored into the dashboard image, under dashboard/static/vendor/sisflow/, rather than into the docs, and it ships under the owner's Apache-2.0 grant (D-063, which answered OQ-25). The check runs only when the board's board/index.js is present, so a tree without the board passes. Positive: sisflow has its licence text and sisflow has its NOTICE — the NOTICE records the grant and the source commit. Negative (TC-310): the board present without either file fails with sisflow is vendored without LICENSE-sisflow.txt or sisflow is vendored without NOTICE-sisflow.txt, and the script exits 1, as for the fonts and the charting library (TC-150/TC-151).

rebuild-restart.sh — prod builds from a tag (TC-152…TC-155)

Until v0.13.0 both tiers built from the working tree, so production tracked whatever happened to be checked out. The --allow-dirty refusal narrowed that and did not close it: a clean tree is not a named tree, and "what is running in prod?" had no answer a month later. --tag <ref> checks the ref out into a detached worktree under .worktrees/ and re-runs the script inside it, so the invariant check, the test gate and docker compose build all read the tagged tree. It works without any path rewriting because both compose files address their build contexts relatively. Positive (TC-152): worktree at v0.12.0 (b4b43bf) -> .worktrees/v0.12.0 — or worktree already at v0.12.0 (b4b43bf) when one is being reused — then env files linked from the working tree (they are not in any commit), because the tier's credentials are created by hand and never committed, so a fresh worktree has none and compose would stop at env file not found; then re-running inside the worktree and, from the second invocation, inside the worktree at v0.12.0 (b4b43bf). The --tag flag is deliberately not forwarded to that second invocation: the script it runs is the tag's own copy of this file, which may predate the flag entirely, and dropping it is what lets --tag build a ref older than the feature. Negative (TC-153): a prod action with neither flag is refused — prod builds from a tag -- refusing to deploy from the working tree, followed by the available tags. Negative (TC-154): an unknown ref is resolved before anything is created, so no such ref: v9.9.9 and nothing was created. `git tag` lists what is available. leave no half-made directory behind. Positive (TC-155): --from-tree is the deliberate escape hatch — building from the working tree (b4b43bf) — and the dirty refusal still guards it with working tree is dirty -- refusing to touch prod. Passing both flags is a usage error: --tag and --from-tree contradict each other. Dev is unchanged and needs no tag, which is the point of dev. Since v0.37.0 (D-072) a prod --tag whose compose mounts the machine API's token files also refuses to start when one is missing: FAIL deploy/stacks/prod.api-qa is missing (or is not a regular file), with create it, mode 600 (empty is fine) -- see compose.prod.yaml -- then re-run., and prints ok the machine API's token files exist otherwise; Docker would otherwise create the missing source as a root-owned directory.

rebuild-restart.sh — the test gate runs with or without a local Go (TC-147, TC-148)

The VM these stacks run on has Docker and no Go toolchain — not on PATH, not under a login shell, not in /usr/local/go. The gate above has no bypass flag, so until v0.11.0 the documented deploy path could not complete on the machine it deploys to, and the workaround was to run docker compose by hand: the one thing .claude/agents/gaos-deploy.md tells the subagent never to do. A gap in the tooling that forces the rule to be broken is a gap in the tooling. Positive (TC-147): the script now picks an arm before the gate and says which, go toolchain: local go1.24.x or go toolchain: docker golang:1.24-alpine, and all three checks run identically either way — the container arm mounts the repo at /src and keeps GOCACHE inside itself, so no build cache lands in the working tree. The image is GAOS_GO_IMAGE, pinned by default. Negative (TC-148): a machine with neither Go nor Docker stops the deploy rather than skipping the gate — no go toolchain and no docker -- the test gate cannot run, followed by install Go, or install Docker so the gate can run in $GO_IMAGE, exit 1. A gate that silently skips itself is worse than one that cannot run, because the output still reads as though the tests were part of the deploy.

rebuild-restart.sh — build and up (TC-030, TC-031, TC-032)

Positive (TC-030): docker compose build $SERVICE and up -d $SERVICE both exit 0, the container list prints, verification begins. Negative (TC-031): the build fails (a broken Dockerfile, a missing build context) → bad "build failed", exit 1, up -d never runs. Negative (TC-032): up -d fails (e.g. a port collision with a stray container) → bad "up -d failed", exit 1.

rebuild-restart.sh — the restart action (TC-033, TC-034)

Positive (TC-033): docker compose restart $SERVICE exits 0 — no build step ever runs for this action — verification begins. Negative (TC-034): restart fails (e.g. the named service isn't currently running) → bad "restart failed", exit 1.

rebuild-restart.sh — single-service scope (TC-035)

Positive: rebuild-restart.sh dev deploy dashboard builds, ups, or restarts only the dashboard service — traefik, landing and docs are untouched, visible in the printed dc ps line as the only service with a fresh timestamp.

rebuild-restart.sh — verification: bind address (TC-036, TC-037)

Positive: ss -ltn shows the port bound to $TUNNEL:$PORT only (TUNNEL is $GAOS_TUNNEL_IP, default 10.10.0.2) → vok "bound to $TUNNEL:$PORT only". Negative: a compose or Traefik regression drops the tunnel prefix, or the entrypoint binds 0.0.0.0 → vbad "unexpected bind: …".

rebuild-restart.sh — verification: healthz (TC-038, TC-039)

Positive: curl through the SSH tunnel with the right Host: header returns a body containing "ok":true → vok "healthz ok:true". Since 2026-11-02 the probe is polled every 3 seconds for up to GAOS_HEALTH_WAIT seconds (default 90), and the body is printed with (after Ns): the dashboard applies its schema over the tunnel to clinodb before it listens, and a single probe straight after up -d reported every healthy deploy as three failures. Negative: the dashboard is crash-looping or Host: routing is misconfigured, so the wait runs out → vbad "healthz did not report ok:true".

rebuild-restart.sh — verification: route status codes (TC-040, TC-041)

Positive: / → 200, /app → 301, /docs → 301, /app/ → 200 — all four vok. Negative: any one path returns the wrong code (e.g. the /app redirect regressed to TC-016's failure mode) → vbad "$path -> $got, want $want" for that path only; the other three still pass independently.

rebuild-restart.sh — verification: base href survival (TC-042, TC-043)

Positive: GET /app/ contains exactly <base href="/app/"> → vok. Negative: the base href is stripped, wrong, or absent — a template regression, or a downstream effect of TC-041's redirect failure — → vbad "base href: '…', want '<base href=\"/app/\">'".

rebuild-restart.sh — verification: --full-verify checks (TC-044, TC-045, TC-046, TC-047)

Only run when --full-verify is passed. TC-044, positive: a request carrying X-Forwarded-For: 203.0.113.9 produces a Traefik access-log line for /api/healthz whose ClientHost is 203.0.113.9 → vok. TC-045, negative: Traefik's trusted-proxy list doesn't include the tunnel hop, so the header is ignored and the log shows the tunnel's own address instead → vbad "ClientHost not 203.0.113.9: …". TC-046, positive: curl https://$HOST/api/healthz from the public edge returns "ok":true within 15 seconds → vok. TC-047, negative: the public path is down — edge nginx misconfigured, an expired certificate, the WireGuard tunnel down — → curl times out or returns a non-JSON body → vbad "public healthz did not report ok:true".

The rest of this section is the two Go test packages. They differ from the shell automations in one way worth stating: a shell check prints ok or FAIL and keeps going, while a Go test fails with a message written for whoever has to fix it. Those messages are what is quoted below — copied from dashboard/main_test.go and pustaka/main_test.go, not reconstructed. One TC- per func Test…, because that is the unit go test -run can actually select; several of them are table-driven and carry both a positive and a negative half, and where that happens both halves are described.

dashboard — routes and rendering (TC-054, TC-055, TC-056, TC-057)

The file's own header explains why these exist: a dashboard has almost no assertions of its own, so a wrong template renders a page that looks fine. Positive (TC-054): TestEveryRouteAnswers walks 32 method/path cases with a back-office cookie — the documented surface (whose desk routes answer JSON since v0.29.0), the back office and every product tab, the wizard — and the negative half of the same table asks for GET /products/nope and five routes of the retired floor, and requires a 404 for each; a mismatch prints %s %s = %d, want %d followed by body: %s. Negative (TC-055): three surfaces are fetched and the body must not contain template error — %s rendered a template error. Negative (TC-056): the style-attribute trap. html/template refuses to interpolate a plain string into a style="" context and substitutes the literal ZgotmplZ; the page still renders and has silently lost every colour, width and role tint, so five surfaces are checked and the failure is %s emitted ZgotmplZ -- a computed style lost its template.CSS type. Negative (TC-057): X-Forwarded-Prefix is attacker-controlled on an unproxied request and its value lands in browser-visible output. Three valid values must produce <base href="/app/"> or <base href="/">; eight hostile ones — https://evil.example, //evil.example, a bare app, a backslash, a quote, a newline, and a 200-character path — must each be refused back to <base href="/"> rather than sanitised into something plausible, or prefix %q: want %s.

dashboard — health (TC-058, TC-059)

Positive (TC-058): with no database configured, /healthz parses as JSON, ok is true and store is memory — that is the local stack, and the failure says so: an unconfigured database must stay a 200: that is the local stack. Negative (TC-059): pointing Arcade at http://127.0.0.1:1 reproduces the failure the deploy check exists to catch — wrong network name, wrong alias, wrong password — and must give a 503: status = %d, want 503, then database = %v, want unreachable.

dashboard — the auth boundary (TC-060, TC-061, TC-062)

The check behind this boundary is a placeholder (see M6.4); its shape is not, and that is what these pin. Negative (TC-060): the test walks all nine guarded routes rather than sampling them, because a route that quietly never had a check is exactly what it exists to find — %s %s = %d, want 401. Negative (TC-061): a login value that is not an email is a 422 carrying Enter the email, or status = %d, want 422 and the error message did not reach the form. Positive (TC-062): the tenant label contains a middle dot, and net/http drops invalid bytes from a cookie value silently, so an unescaped value is a truncated header rather than an error; the cookie must round-trip to loop-eng · a@b.c, or no tenant cookie was set / round-trip = %q.

dashboard — floor mutations and their guards (TC-063, TC-064, TC-065, TC-149)

Negative (TC-063): approving a desk with no gate must be rejected. Negative (TC-064): architecture §06 sets max_retry_count at 3 per task, and the guardrail belongs in the store rather than the UI because the UI is not the only caller it will ever have — three retries succeed, then the fourth retry must be refused, and the error text must contain guard or the refusal must say why: %v. Positive (TC-065): cancelling releases the desk, or state=%s agents=%v, want idle with no agents. Positive (TC-149): the same approval, but on a floor rebuilt from the graph rather than seeded from fixtures. ArcadeStore.load() wrote its own MemStore literal and left rng nil, because nothing in the load path reads it — but advance() does, through pickTask, and ApproveTask calls advance(). So a dashboard restarted against a populated database segfaulted the first time an operator released a merge gate, and every store the conformance suite builds goes through NewMemStore, which sets rng, so nothing caught it. The case builds the store the way load() does and drives that path: approving a gate on a rebuilt floor: %v, then releasing a merge gate must emit at least ApprovalGranted, then advance must have seated a task; a nil rng used to panic here instead. The fix is one construction path — newProjection() — because a projection is a store with no fixtures in it, not a store with no fields.

dashboard — the readiness rubric and the planning floor (TC-156…TC-160)

BRD §8.2 specifies the rubric as twelve machine-checkable signals worth 100 points, and BR-07 makes 45 a refusal line rather than a label. Until v0.13.0 dashboard/model.go scored seven booleans and marked itself as the stand-in, and the planner planned whatever it was given. Both failures are quiet ones: a rubric whose weights no longer sum to 100 still returns a plausible number and still sorts records, and a planner that plans an empty brief returns a plan that is confidently wrong. Positive (TC-156): the weights are the document's, not ours — the rubric has %d signals, want 12 and the twelve weights sum to %d, want 100 — and every signal must carry the line the author is shown when it fails, or signal %q carries no missing-list line. Positive (TC-157): the honest ceiling is 95, not 100. Eleven signals can be earned today; §8.2's verification strategy asks how correctness is proven and the in-memory record has nowhere to record the answer, so the signal is listed, scores zero and says why rather than being dropped — a complete record scores %d, want 95, 95 is band %q, want complete, a complete record is missing %d signals, want 1, and the one deferred signal should be verification strategy, got %q. A rubric that quietly removes the signal it cannot measure is how a bar moves without anyone voting on it. Negative (TC-158): the other end — an empty record scores %d, want 0, 0 is band %q, want insufficient, an empty record is missing %d signals, want all 12. Negative (TC-159): criteria coverage is the signal most likely to be softened into partial credit, so it is pinned all-or-nothing. One must story with a single criterion forfeits all twelve points — one must story with a single criterion must forfeit the whole signal and losing coverage costs the full 12 points: scored %d, want 83 — and a record with no stories at all must not collect them by vacuous truth, which is the quiet way an empty brief scores well: a record with no stories must not earn coverage by vacuous truth. Negative (TC-160): BR-07 itself. The draft record p-5 scores 35, and planning it must fail rather than produce the stage template — planned %d tasks for a record scoring %d; BR-07 refuses below %d, with a refused plan must be nil, not a half-built one. The refusal must satisfy ErrRejected, so every handler that already turns a rejection into a toast keeps working (a refusal is a rejection, not a failure: %v), and it must carry the list, because a refusal that does not say what is missing is a dead end (the refusal must carry the missing list, got %T, a refusal that does not say what is missing is a dead end, the refusal reports %d/%s, the record scores %d/%s). Finally a floor that never lets anything through is a wall, so the filled record still plans: p-1 scores %d and must still plan: %v. The rule lives in MemStore.PlanWorkflow, which is also what ArcadeStore plans through, so there is one implementation and the two stores cannot disagree about it.

dashboard — the pre-dispatch egress pass (TC-161…TC-175)

L-4 specifies a pass that runs on every context slice before the model sees anything, and BR-32 makes a positive detection refuse the dispatch rather than report the leak afterwards. R-11 is what that is for: egress is "automatic, continuous, and widens on every retry ... correct behaviour, unreviewed content, irreversible send." The pass is a pure function, so every case below is a table entry rather than a scenario. Positive (TC-161): the verdict becomes an event and the reducer folds it, so two screens of one packet must agree — Screen returned a different verdict on the second call with identical input, and the message goes on to say why: "a pass that is not a function of its arguments makes a replay disagree with the run". Positive (TC-162): a control that never lets anything through is not a control — a slice with nothing to find was refused: rule=%q reason=%q — a pass that never lets anything through is not a control, it is an outage, and the cleared packet must carry a digest, because 04-execution.sql declares ContextSlice.digest and an empty one proves nothing. Negative (TC-163): seven real credential shapes — an Anthropic key, an AWS access key id, a private-key block, a GitHub token, a password assignment, a JWT and a URL carrying basic auth — each must refuse: a %s was in the slice and Screen allowed the dispatch; L-4 says a positive detection refuses BEFORE the model sees anything, and a secret scored afterwards has already left the building. Negative (TC-164): the most dangerous thing this file could do is record what it found. the verdict for detector %q contains the matched text; a DispatchRefused event is append-only (BR-04) and rendered to a browser, so this writes the credential permanently into the record that exists to prove it never left. Negative (TC-165) and Positive (TC-166): the allowlist and the detectors sit on opposite sides of one line. A path outside the allowlist is dropped — the allowlist bounds what leaves, it does not abort — the part is dropped and ContextRedacted records it — and a key inside a file that was dropped is therefore not a refusal at all: a key inside a dropped .env refused the dispatch: rule=%q; the file never left, so it is a redaction — refusing here would make every repository with a .env undispatchable. Negative (TC-167): %q cleaned to a path outside the workspace and the pass allowed it; a path that escapes the workspace is a refusal, or an allowlist is a suggestion. Positive (TC-168): the bug the standard library hands you for free — matchGlob(%q, %q) = %v, want %v; path.Match's * never crosses a separator, so "src/**" that matched only "src/a" would be a policy that looks configured and protects nothing. Negative (TC-169, TC-170): the whole-slice limit refuses and the per-file limit drops, and collapsing the two would lose the distinction — truncating makes the frozen packet a lie about what the model received (D-051), so the limit refuses. Negative (TC-171): L-6's no-egress mode — local_only is set and target %q is not local, and Screen allowed it; L-6 fails the task rather than downgrading quietly — and the other half, that it still permits a local model. Negative (TC-172): the reported reason is a fixed ranking rather than whichever check ran first: a slice that was both oversized and carried a credential was refused with rule %q; the operator must be told about the key, not the byte count. Positive (TC-173): the two defaults differ and a reader will assume they match, so both are pinned — an empty Redact list is a scanner nobody armed, and the default for a forgotten security control is on against an empty AllowPaths is an allowlist nobody wrote, which cannot be a list of files somebody meant to exclude. Negative (TC-174): every part was redacted and the dispatch went ahead with an empty slice; spending an attempt on a packet with nothing in it burns a retry against BR-17's guard and teaches the reviewer nothing. Negative (TC-175): the gate is a type — an adapter takes a Cleared, and only the pass returns one — but everything here is one Go package, so the type stops a caller forgetting and not a caller deciding. The deliberate version is caught by walking the package's syntax tree: %s:%d constructs a populated Cleared outside egress.go; BR-32 says nothing reaches a provider without the egress pass, and the only reason that is true is that Screen is the only thing that makes the argument Dispatch takes.

dashboard — the handoff document (TC-176…TC-180)

BR-30 requires a handoff document in the declared shape at the declared path, and D-050 promoted it from a convenience to the thing correctness rests on. §7.6 calls it a contract rather than a convention: "an agent that writes prose where a parser expects a field has failed the attempt." Positive (TC-176): the shape printed in the BRD parses field for field — field %s parsed as %q, want %q; the handoff is a contract, not a convention, and a parser that is lenient about the shape is a chain that degrades quietly, plus parsed %d acceptance criteria, want 2; no criterion means no verdict, and the reviewer will invent one and criterion 0 evidence is %q; the evidence is what makes a met criterion checkable rather than claimed. Negative (TC-177): a document with no front matter parsed successfully; §7.6 says an agent that writes prose where a parser expects a field has failed the attempt. Negative (TC-178): a missing field and an invented status are the same failure — a handoff with no run id and no criteria was accepted; all four ways this fails — absent, no front matter, unparseable, incomplete — are one failure to the retry loop and carry one signature, and status "probably-fine" was accepted; the contract declares complete|blocked|partial and a fourth value is a document GAOS cannot score. Positive (TC-179): the usage block is optional and its absence is not a zero — the absent usage block parsed as %+v, want nil; nil is what makes the basis unavailable rather than a zero somebody invented. Negative (TC-180): a workspace with no handoff document read successfully; BR-30 says a missing one is a failed attempt, never a silent pass. Retired in v0.29.0 with the CLI execution layer (M14.7); see TC-281.

dashboard — the version pin and the boot smoke test (TC-181…TC-185)

D-049 pins the first supported adapter at @anthropic-ai/claude-code@2.1.238 and leaves one obligation: "the pin is worthless without the boot smoke test §7.6.3 already promises, and that lands with T-057 rather than before it." The comparison is pure and tested against captured output; the exec half is TC-192. Positive (TC-181): ParseVersion(%q) = %v, want %v; this is the captured output of the version D-049 pins, and the parser reading it wrong is how a correct install refuses to boot. Retired in v0.29.0 with the CLI execution layer (M14.7); see TC-283. Negative (TC-182): ParseVersion(%q) returned %v with no error; a zero version compares as below the pin, which sends the operator hunting a drift that is really a changed banner — and against a pin with no minimum it would silently pass. Retired in v0.29.0 with the CLI execution layer (M14.7); see TC-283. Negative (TC-183): both edges, and the closed grammar — pin %q permits %v = %v, want %v; >= is inclusive and < is exclusive, and a pin that got either edge wrong would look right for every version except the two that matter, with ParsePin(%q) succeeded; the grammar is >=x.y.z and <x.y.z and nothing else, because an operator guessing which one ~ implements has pinned something other than what they meant. Retired in v0.29.0 with the CLI execution layer (M14.7); see TC-283. Negative (TC-184): a tool reporting %v against pin %q started the dashboard; §7.6.3 pins the CLI because flags and output formats move with no deprecation window, and an adapter on an unmeasured version produces wrong evidence rather than an error, and the refusal must name all three of the tool, the version and the pin, or the operator cannot tell a drift from a missing binary. Retired in v0.29.0 with the CLI execution layer (M14.7); see TC-283. Positive (TC-185): an unconfigured adapter is a valid state — an empty ModelConfig gave (%v, %v), want (nil, nil); an upgrade that refused to boot until a new variable was set would take every running dashboard down — while the retired mode, a half-configured gateway and an unbuilt API mode all refuse. Since v0.29.0 the case lives in adapter_guard_test.go: GAOS_MODEL_MODE=cli gave %v; the CLI path was retired (D-055) and must refuse the boot saying so, gateway mode with no URL built an adapter; a half-configured gateway is a deployment error, not a run to fail later, and mode api built an adapter; T-058 is not built, and an operator who asked for a metered adapter must not silently get another.

dashboard — the CLI adapter, and the seam guards that outlived it (TC-186…TC-193)

T-057, run against a stand-in tool in testdata/fakecli rather than a real provider: no network call, no credential and no cost in go test. The fake exists to reproduce the finding behind D-050, which is the one that makes this seam's shape necessary. Negative (TC-186): the measurement was that Claude Code separates a crash from a completed run but not a refusal from a success. So the fake exits 0 having written status: blocked, and the adapter must still say blocked — the tool exited %d and wrote status %q, and the attempt reports outcome %q — D-050: the exit code may classify crash versus ran and nothing else, because Claude Code exits 0 with terminal_reason: completed for a task it could not do. Retired in v0.29.0 with the CLI execution layer (M14.7); see TC-280. Negative (TC-187): exit 0 with nothing written, and exit 0 with prose written, are one failure — the tool exited 0 and wrote no handoff document, and the attempt came back as %q with signature %q; BR-30 says missing or unparseable is a failed attempt with the signature handoff-missing, never a silent pass, and splitting the class would give the guard two counters where BR-17 specifies one. Retired in v0.29.0 with the CLI execution layer (M14.7); see TC-281. Positive (TC-188): the document is read even after a bad exit — a non-zero exit skipped the handoff read; the document is the authority whatever the process did, and a run killed after writing it still said what happened. Retired in v0.29.0 with the CLI execution layer (M14.7); see TC-280. Positive (TC-189): D-051's closing measurement was that a default invocation inherited the host's configuration and roughly 24k cache-read tokens. So the child's environment is built rather than inherited — the child inherited a host variable nobody named; D-051 measured 24k cache-read tokens arriving this way, which makes the frozen slice a lie about what the model received — and the named session variables must still arrive, or the tool cannot find its own session. Retired in v0.29.0 with the CLI execution layer (M14.7). Positive (TC-190): cost comes from the tool's own report — the tool reported total_cost_usd and the attempt carries %v/%q; D-051 says the first adapter reports, so the basis is measured from the first run — cache reads included, because the cache-read count is real spend the operator was billed for, and dropping it under-reports every run; and a silent tool gives %q, want %q; BR-37 and D-044 forbid an unpriceable figure being reported as zero or dressed up as an estimate. Retired in v0.29.0 with the CLI execution layer (M14.7); see TC-275 and TC-282. Negative (TC-191): since v0.29.0 against the gateway adapter, in adapter_guard_test.go — the adapter dispatched a zero-value Cleared; BR-32 says nothing reaches a model provider without passing the egress check first, and a type is only most of that guarantee, and the fake gateway must have received nothing: a zero-value Cleared reached the gateway as %d request(s). Positive (TC-192): the exec half of the smoke test, both ways — a tool outside the pin passed the boot smoke test; the pin is inert without it, which is exactly what D-049 said the obligation was. Retired in v0.29.0 with the CLI execution layer (M14.7); see TC-283. Negative (TC-193): BR-33 made checkable rather than trusted, by the same syntax-tree walk TC-175 uses: %s:%d branches on the invocation mode outside the adapter; BR-33 says the mode may be recorded and never branched on, and TaskRun.invocation exists so that prohibition can be checked rather than trusted. Since v0.24.0 the walk also treats InvocationGateway as a mode constant. Since v0.29.0 it lives in adapter_guard_test.go and exempts exactly the seam — adapter.go, adapter_aimanager.go, modelconfig.go and the two test files that drive it.

dashboard — the dispatch route and its two events (TC-194…TC-200)

T-127. L-5 requires that redactions and refusals "appear in the timeline like any other state change", and an event with no emitter appears nowhere — which is why these run through MemStore.DispatchTask rather than through the pass. The simulator is pointedly not the emitter: dashboard/events.go calls emitting a catalog event from sim.go inventing evidence. Positive (TC-194): a dispatch that cleared the egress pass emitted %v; the stream is the record (BR-04) and a dispatch nothing wrote never happened, and the event carries the digest, because an attempt that does not name its input cannot be audited against one. Negative (TC-195): the one surprising line in the method, pinned so nobody “fixes” it — DispatchTask returned err=%v on a refusal; a refusal is a recorded state change, and returning ErrRejected means the two events L-5 requires are written to the stream and never reach the response that renders them. Negative (TC-196): the desk is %q after a refused dispatch, want blocked — a desk left idle says nothing happened, and something did, plus a refused dispatch did not raise the attention count (%d -> %d); stats() counts blocked desks, and a refusal nobody is told about is indistinguishable from a broken filter, and the payload's rule, because a reducer that had to parse the sentence would break the first time somebody improved the wording. This case is why TC-139 in dashboard/state_test.go changed: idle → blocked was asserted illegal with the reason "nothing is running, so nothing can be blocked", and BR-32 created the counter-example. The row is inverted rather than deleted. Positive (TC-197): the repository parts come through the same seam as TC-200's — a slice with a part cut dispatched without a ContextRedacted: %v; L-5 exists because an invisible filter is indistinguishable from a broken one. Positive (TC-198): two brand-new event types are the likeliest place for the reducer's contract to lapse — folding the stream twice put desk %s in %q then %q; the reducer is required to be pure and order-stable, and a refusal the reducer does not reproduce is a refusal that vanishes on restart. Negative (TC-199): the read-only handle dispatched a task; the floor is parked in the past, and a dispatch from there would send a slice assembled out of a historical record. Negative (TC-200): the end-to-end version of R-11, with a real key in a repository file. Since v0.29.0 GAOS reads no folder on its host, so the file reaches the packet through the store's repository seam (repoParts, where M1.2's index and the Blueprint's knowledge sources will plug in) rather than off a disk; the pass it meets is the same — a workspace carrying a credential was dispatched; BR-32 says a detection refuses the dispatch, and the safety score in domain E cannot recall a sent slice, and the refusal pill contains the credential; the event stream is append-only and rendered to a browser, so this writes the key permanently into the record that exists to prove it never left.

dashboard — store isolation, the unbounded stream and replay (TC-066…TC-070)

Negative (TC-066): a snapshot that shares its slices with the live floor is a replay that keeps changing under the operator, so Clone is tested directly rather than through a handler — mutating a snapshot changed the store. Negative (TC-067): this case asserted the opposite until v0.11.0. It required the stream to stay capped at a hundred and sixty entries, which is what BR-04 says an append-only record must not be, and T-013 removed the ring. It now requires that nothing is ever dropped: after four hundred ticks the oldest event is still genesis (oldest event is %s, want FloorOpened -- the front of the stream was dropped) at sequence one (oldest event has seq %d, want 1), with no hole anywhere behind it (gap in the stream: seq %d follows %d). Genesis is still reachable (replay to genesis: %v) and replaying past the end must still fail: replaying past the end must be a not-found, not a silent clamp. Negative (TC-068): twenty ticks while an operator is replaying must not move the floor — the floor advanced while an operator was replaying it. Positive (TC-069): forty ticks must produce events and spend, or 40 ticks emitted no events and 40 ticks cost nothing. Negative (TC-070): a paused floor advanced its clock.

dashboard — the master record (TC-071…TC-074)

Positive (TC-071): the seeded record projects to 1 + 7 + 4 + 10 + 2 + 2 + 2 vertices and three of its four stories carry a task, so coverage = %d, want 75.
