Negative (TC-073): a story with no role must be refused. Negative (TC-074): mutating a returned Master changed the store.

dashboard — planning and the wizard (TC-075…TC-081)

Positive (TC-075): a plan is eight tasks — planner, architect, backend and QA twice, security, devops. It must start planner then architect (a plan always starts planner then architect); every DEPENDS_ON must point at a task earlier in the plan, which is the acyclicity invariant from the ArcadeDB model §11 (%s depends on %s, which is not earlier in the plan); and the last task must be the gated devops one running the top tier (the gated task is the last one, and it is devops, then a gated task runs on the top tier, got %s against claude-opus-5). Positive (TC-076): a record with no stories still follows the six-task stage template — retired 2026-08-20. That case asserted the absence of BR-07: a record scoring below 45 is precisely what the planner must now refuse, so the behaviour it pinned was the gap, not a feature. Its replacement is TC-160, and the row is struck rather than renumbered. Positive (TC-077): planning a story that had no tasks must raise coverage: US-04 had no tasks; planning it must raise coverage (%d -> %d). Negative (TC-078): a workflow with no objective must be refused. Positive (TC-079): the wizard's draft round-trips through hidden form fields, and if that breaks, step 3 silently plans against the wrong product — the body must still carry name="product" value="p-1" and both story ids, or the draft lost %s. Negative (TC-080): advancing without a product is status = %d, want 422 and the body must carry Pick the product, or the refusal must reach the operator as a toast. Positive (TC-081): going back must never be blocked by the field you went back to fix — going back was blocked: %d.

dashboard — the sequence renderer and the helpers (TC-082…TC-086)

dashboard/seq.go builds SVG by hand, so every value that reaches it must be escaped by hand too — it is the one place in the codebase where html/template is not doing that work. Positive (TC-082): a two-line source renders <svg class="seq", both participants, the message text and class="arrow ret" for the dashed return, or missing %q in plus the SVG. Negative (TC-083): a source with no arrow falls back to <pre> — a source with no arrow is shown as text, got %q. Negative (TC-084): a source containing <script> in both the participant and the message must not reach the output unescaped: unescaped markup reached the SVG:. Positive (TC-085): splitLines drops blanks and trims — got %q. Positive (TC-086): slug(%q) = %q, want %q, over Route Planner, a double-spaced string and Ops/Warehouse.

dashboard — the graph schema and its two reconciliations (TC-109…TC-112)

The DDL in dashboard/schema/ is extracted from the data model document and reconciled against ArcadeDB 26.7.2; schema/RECONCILIATION.md records both deviations and how to reproduce the measurement. These four cases need no database — they guard the text, because the live failures they stand in for are slow and quiet. Positive (TC-109): the counts the model document states about itself must hold — 28, 3 and 37 since 11-blueprint.sql added Policy, KnowledgeSource, BlueprintVersion and five edges (§15) — or %s types: got %d, want %d (the model document's own count) -- %v; the two supertypes are excluded and their absence is fatal on its own (supertype %s is not declared; every concrete type EXTENDS it), and a repeated declaration fails with type %s is declared more than once. Negative (TC-110): D-045 replaced the UNIQUE keyword — which 26.7.2 rejects outright — with a unique index on the endpoints, and that substitution is the one most likely to rot silently, since duplicate edges do not raise, they accumulate: %d edge types have no UNIQUE index on (`@out`, `@in`), so duplicate edges between the same pair will accumulate silently: %v -- see schema/RECONCILIATION.md deviation 1. The keyword coming back is caught separately — %s: `CREATE EDGE TYPE ... UNIQUE` is not valid on ArcadeDB 26.7.2 and fails the whole file: %s. Negative (TC-111): deploy/schema/apply.sh promises a second run is a no-op, which is what makes it safe to point at dev or prod, and that promise rests entirely on the clause: %s: declaration without IF NOT EXISTS breaks re-application: %s. 10-storage.sql is asserted to be ALTER-only for the same reason, since an ALTER has no such clause to carry — 10-storage.sql declares %d types; it is meant to be ALTER-only, and an ALTER carries no IF NOT EXISTS to protect it. Positive (TC-112): a guard on the other three, which would otherwise pass vacuously over an empty directory — no schema file numbered %s; apply.sh runs them in filename order and a gap means a dependency was skipped.

dashboard — the ArcadeDB query path and the embedded schema (TC-113…TC-123)

T-007 gave dashboard/arcade.go a query path; before it, the only thing in this repository that could speak SQL to ArcadeDB was deploy/schema/apply.sh, in shell. These eleven cases run against an httptest server that speaks the same envelope, so they need no database; the live version of the same assertions is TC-125. Positive (TC-113): a read must reach the read-only endpoint, or a read went to %q, want /api/v1/query/gaos -- the command endpoint would accept a write from a path that is meant to be read-only; and the values must travel as parameters rather than be pasted into the statement, since a product goal containing an apostrophe would otherwise be a syntax error and rather worse than that — the parameter was interpolated into the statement text. Positive (TC-114): the write path is the other endpoint — a write went to %q, want /api/v1/command/gaos. Negative (TC-115): every SQL failure on this server is an HTTP 500, so the status says only that something went wrong and the body says what: the message reads %q; it must carry the detail rather than "Error on transaction commit", which names no cause. Negative (TC-116): IF NOT EXISTS is on every schema statement, but ArcadeDB still reports some re-declarations as errors, and treating those as failures would make the idempotence this project relies on unverifiable — AlreadyExists() is %v for %s, want %v. Negative (TC-117): an unset GAOS_ARCADE_URL is the local stack and a valid state, so a statement must refuse rather than dial nothing (Query on an unconfigured store returned %v, want ErrUnconfigured) while the probe keeps answering as before — Probe reported (%q, %v), want ("unconfigured", nil). Positive (TC-118): an event write allocates a sequence number, writes the event and writes the state together, so a statement that lost the session would leave a state change that emitted no event: a statement inside the transaction carried no arcadedb-session-id; it ran outside the transaction it was meant to be in. Negative (TC-119): the same failure at the other end — Begin accepted a response with no session id; every statement after it would silently run outside the transaction. Negative (TC-120): store.go put a context on every method for this reason — the query took %s to give up; store.go put a context on every method so a slow database cannot hold a request open. Positive (TC-121): the deployed dashboard runs on distroless with no shell, so it applies the DDL from a //go:embed copy rather than from the script. A pattern that stopped matching a file would leave the binary applying a schema one file short and reporting success: the binary embeds %d schema files and %s holds %d: %v vs %v -- the //go:embed pattern and the directory have drifted apart, and byte-level drift is caught by %s differs between the embedded copy and the file on disk. Positive (TC-122): two readers of the same DDL must agree on what a statement is, or the script and the binary would disagree about whether a schema had been applied — split into %d statements, want %d: %q. Negative (TC-123): the negative half over the real files, since a statement that came out empty or still carrying a comment marker is one the database cannot parse — %s: a statement still carries a comment marker: %s, %s: an empty statement survived the split, and the embedded schema split into %d statements; the DDL is a few hundred and a number this low means a file stopped being read.

dashboard — one conformance suite, both stores (TC-124…TC-126)

T-008 asks for MemStore to stay the test double and for both implementations to run against one interface suite, and the implementation plan gives the reason: running both implementations against one interface suite is what stops the seam from rotting. dashboard/store_conformance_test.go holds forty-eight cases — since v0.33.0 including the vendor policy's round trip, since v0.40.0 a manual runner's pass (AManualRunnerReadiesAndNeverDispatches, D-080: whatever the scheduler names is READY, nothing is DISPATCHED or RUNNING, and the fake gateway receives no turn), since v0.41.0 a planned task's timing (CardTimingOfAPlannedTask, D-081: known, complete, opened on its plan in PENDING, and an unknown card is not found), since v0.42.0 a conversation's archive round trip (ConversationArchiveRoundTrip, D-082: a card with no attempt is refused, an unknown card is not found, a fresh card's conversation is active and empty), since v0.44.0 a send-back's round trip (ASendBackRoundTrips, D-084: the design READY, the review PENDING with no reason, two send-back events, a second send-back refused, and in the graph store the actor and the triage survive a reopen) — each taking a Store and nothing else — a constraint that is doing real work, because it is what stops a case reaching for MemStore.Tick(), which is not on the interface. The four tick-driven tests in main_test.go stay where they are for the same reason: they test the simulation, which is one store's private business — and since v0.29.0 a test-only one, in sim_test.go. The speed, pause and focus cases left the table with the floor controls they tested, which are no longer on the interface. Positive (TC-124) and (TC-125): the same table runs twice. Both stores start from the same fixtures, so a case that passes against one and fails against the other is a real disagreement rather than a difference in seed data. Every rejection is compared against its exact wording — rejection reads %q, want %q — and against the shape §09 requires: got %v, want an ErrRejected -- §09 says a mutation that will not happen says why, in a form the handler can turn into a toast. The ArcadeDB half skips rather than fails when no database is configured, because go test ./... has always run anywhere in this repository and rebuild-restart.sh gates every deploy on it: GAOS_ARCADE_TEST_URL is unset; run deploy/schema/scratch.sh up for the ArcadeDB half. Each case gets its own tenant inside one database, which is also a standing check on the tenant scoping — a case that leaked would see another case's desks. Positive (TC-126): the case that cannot be written against the interface, because “restart the process” is not something a Store exposes. It is also the one that decides whether the in-memory projection is a cache or a lie, since BR-01 allows anything cached in memory that is reconstructible: reopened floor has %d desks, the graph was written with %d -- the projection is holding state the database never received, and reopened store has %d events, wrote %d -- BR-02 says a state change that emitted no event never happened, and half of these did not.

dashboard — the fold test (TC-050, TC-051, TC-131, TC-132)

This is the acceptance test BRD §4.1 has been waiting on since the BRD was written, and it ran for the first time on 2026-08-17. It was a forward specification on this page until then, under not yet implemented; the specification has not changed, only its section. Architecture §08 states the target exactly — state(t) = fold(reduce, events.filter(e => e.ts <= t), snapshot_before(t) ?? empty) — and gives the reason snapshots cannot be the source: if the UI can only replay from stored snapshots, then any moment between two snapshots is unreachable and any bug in snapshot writing is invisible.

Positive (TC-050): the run is driven past two rungs of the snapshot ladder, then fold-from-genesis and fold-from-nearest-snapshot are compared at every rung and at moments between them — between is where a broken ladder hides, because a snapshot only ever asked for its own sequence is never asked to be folded forward from. A disagreement is a release blocker, not a warning: at seq %d, fold-from-genesis and fold-from-nearest-snapshot produced different floors; divergence proves the event model is incomplete. The run first proves the ladder exists at all, so the test cannot pass by comparing a fold from genesis against a fold from genesis: %d events produced %d snapshot rungs; the ladder is not being built. Positive (TC-131): the fold is then held against the live floor, field by field, for everything an event is responsible for — desk %s folded to state %q, live is %q, desk %s folded to gate=%v, live is %v, desk %s folded to retry=%d esc=%d, live is retry=%d esc=%d, and the same for score, stage, task and agents. This is the case that decides whether BR-02 actually holds: a state change that emitted no event is one the fold cannot know about, and the two floors part company. Negative (TC-051): the comparison has to be capable of failing, or passing it means nothing. The last event that moved a desk is deleted from the stream and the fold must notice — dropping the %s at seq %d did not change the folded floor; the comparison cannot fail, so passing it means nothing. The last rather than the first, deliberately: events carry absolute values, so an early one is usually overwritten by a later one on the same desk and dropping it correctly changes nothing. Negative (TC-132): the entire ladder is then thrown away and the identical floor demanded, which is BR-21 stated as a test — discarding every snapshot changed the folded floor; the snapshots have become the source of truth and the event stream has stopped being it.

dashboard — the reducer is pure, total and order-stable (TC-133…TC-137)

Three properties, each named in §08 and each tested rather than asserted in a comment. Negative (TC-133) — total: events carrying a type this build has never heard of are interleaved through the whole stream, with payloads designed to do damage if anyone read them, and the folded floor must be unchanged: an unknown event type changed the folded floor; reduce must skip what it does not recognise, not guess at it. The scenario is not hypothetical — the catalog is closed but not frozen, D-037 added three names to it in this very release, and a rolling upgrade means one build reading another's stream. Positive (TC-134) — pure: folding the same stream twice gives the same answer (folding the same stream twice gave two answers) and the result does not alias the state it was folded from (the fold handed back a floor aliasing its own base state). Positive (TC-135) — order-stable: the same events shuffled into a different order fold to the same floor, because ties are broken by (ts, seq) — the same events in a different order folded to a different floor; §08 requires ties broken by (ts, seq) so a replay is the same replay every time. Positive (TC-136): every event in the stream is a moment replay can reach, genesis included, which the 160-entry ring made impossible: replay to seq %d (%s): %v. Positive (TC-137): the deltas have to survive the database, not just the process that wrote them. A store is written through, reopened on the same tenant, and the reloaded stream folded — every event came back from the graph with an empty payload; the reducer deltas are not being persisted and a replay would fold to a blank floor, then desk %s: the graph says %q, the reloaded stream folds to %q. Skips with the rest of the ArcadeDB half when no database is configured.

dashboard — the task state machine (TC-138…TC-142)

Build-order step 2, and the half of BR-06 that was missing: Result and reject() gave the shape, but transitions were enforced by whichever code path happened to run rather than by a boundary that refuses an illegal one. dashboard/state.go is that boundary and every write to Desk.State goes through it. Positive (TC-138): every move the table declares is taken, and a state may always go to itself — the table declares %s -> %s and canTransition refuses it, and %s -> %s refused; a mutation that leaves the state alone is still a mutation, and every call site would need its own exception. A machine that refuses legal work is as broken as one that permits illegal work, and it is the failure a transition table gets wrong most often. Negative (TC-139): sixteen named illegal moves, each with the reason it is illegal written beside it — idle to review (an empty desk has produced nothing to review), review to blocked (review is not waiting on a dependency, it is waiting on a verdict), deferred to blocked (BR-34: a deferral is not a failure and not a blocker) and the rest — all reported as %s -> %s is permitted; %s. Moves in and out of a state nobody declared are refused too. Negative (TC-140): the refusal has to explain itself and has to not happen. An illegal transition returns an ErrRejected reading A task cannot go from idle to completed. and leaves the desk where it was (a refused transition still moved the desk to %q); an undeclared target says the name is unknown instead ("nonsense" is not a task state.), because those two need different messages or the first person to hit one goes looking in the wrong place. Positive (TC-141): every state a live desk reaches and every state the reducer can produce is one the table declares — desk %s is in state %q, which the transition table does not declare and the fold put desk %s in state %q, which is not a declared state. A desk in an undeclared state would be stuck, and the reason would read like a typo. Negative (TC-142): end to end through the seam, a retry or a cancel on an idle desk is refused with a sentence — %s on an idle desk returned %v, want ErrRejected and %s refused without saying why.

dashboard — the closed event catalog (TC-143, TC-144, TC-145, TC-146)

BR-05 closes the catalog: a new event type is a schema change to the fold, not a free addition. Positive (TC-143): a full run — the simulator, plus the operator-driven mutations it never reaches by itself — is checked against the model document's list, written out in the test file independently of dashboard/events.go. Deriving it from the constants would make the test agree with the code by construction, which is the one thing it must not do: event seq %d was written as %q, which is in neither the model document's catalog nor the UI-lifecycle block. The run must be broad enough to mean something — only %d distinct event types were emitted; the run is not exercising enough of the catalog for this to mean anything. Negative (TC-144): the declared-but-unwritten names are pinned to the modules that owe them — the review engine (M5.2), the six guards (M5.5), the planner's task graph (M3.2), recall (M7.3) and the budget guard (M10.3). BlueprintPublished joined the catalog in v0.24.0 already emitted, never owed: the driver saves a policy and publishes a Blueprint, so the name lands with something that writes it. QuestionAsked and QuestionAnswered joined in v0.31.0 the same way: the runner driver also supervises a turn that asks and answers it, so both names land written. The whole execution group came off that list on 2026-08-22, which is what this case is for: the driver now runs one real supervision and one boot recovery — since v0.29.0 a gateway turn against the fake ai-manager and a request the gateway answers 404 for, past its deadline — because a name is only emitted if something emits it and a test that just deleted four rows from the map would be recording an intention. TaskOrphaned needed the second driver rather than the first, and the reason is the point: no live supervisor ever witnesses an orphan, because an orphan is a run whose supervisor is already gone. A name that starts being emitted fails (%s is being emitted, but this list still says it is owed to %s -- if the module landed, take it out of the list) and so does a declared name with no reason beside it (%s is in the catalog, is not emitted, and is not listed as owed to a later module; a declared name nothing writes needs a reason next to it). Emitting a Heartbeat nobody sent, or a RunExited from a process that never started, would be inventing evidence in the one place that must not. Negative (TC-145): BR-10 makes a downward band crossing an event rather than a silent recalculation. A record is filled in, injected, gutted and re-injected, and the drop must be announced — readiness fell from %s (%d) to %s (%d) and no ReadinessDropped was emitted; BR-10 calls that a silent recalculation. Driving it back up must not produce one: readiness went back up and ReadinessDropped was emitted anyway. Positive (TC-146): D-037 adds three event names, one task state and two TaskRun properties, and the BRD is explicit that none of it should be done piecemeal. So they are asserted in one test rather than five, because five would let four pass while one was missing: Task.status permits %q, which has no DEFERRED -- model.go declares StateDeferred and state.go declares its transitions, so a task that reaches it could not be written down, and %s is not declared with a regexp; D-037 lands it with its vocabulary or not at all.

check-stacks.sh — both tiers run the graph store (TC-127, TC-128)

Since M0.2, dev and prod both set GAOS_STORE: "arcadedb". This is the one invariant on the list that is invisible from outside: a tier that fell back to the in-memory fixtures would still serve a floor, still answer /healthz with ok:true, and lose every write on restart — which is precisely what BR-01 is about. Positive (TC-127): the compose files as committed — dev runs the arcadedb store and prod runs the arcadedb store. Negative (TC-128): the setting is removed from a tier, or changed to anything else, and the same two lines print with FAIL instead. The binary itself is the second line of defence and refuses to start rather than falling back, which is the failure this check exists to catch before a deploy rather than during one.

scratch.sh — the throwaway database for the conformance suite (TC-129, TC-130)

The ArcadeDB half of the store suite needs a reachable database, and deploy/schema/scratch.sh is the one command that produces one. It is deliberately throwaway — --rm, no volume, a port of its own — because nothing in dashboard/schema/ should ever be pointed first at an instance anyone depends on. Positive (TC-129): up reports started arcadedata/arcadedb:26.7.2 as gaos-scratch-db on http://localhost:2481, waits for the probe (ready probe answers), applies the DDL and echoes apply.sh's own last line, then prints the two export lines the suite reads. Measured on 2026-08-17, after D-037 took the DDL to 323 statements: 323 applied, 0 already present, 0 failed on a fresh database, and 0 failed on a second run. Negative (TC-130): status or env against nothing prints gaos-scratch-db is not running and exits 1; a port already in use gives could not start arcadedata/arcadedb:26.7.2 on port 2481 with the remedy (is something already bound to 2481? set GAOS_SCRATCH_PORT to move it); a probe that never answers gives http://localhost:2481/api/v1/ready never answered 204; and an unknown action prints the usage and exits 2.

pustaka — the docs checker (TC-087, TC-088, TC-104)

This is the checker every page on this site is validated by, including this one. Negative (TC-087): seven sub-cases run check over a generated fixture and compare the failure count — check() = %d, want %d. Three must pass: omitted metadata, a bounded version range, an open one. Four must fail with exactly one problem: updated before published, bad hashtag (Not Valid, which is neither lowercase nor kebab-case), reversed version (1.0.0 → 0.9.x) and duplicate item (guide, Guide, caught case-insensitively). Negative (TC-088): 0.6.0 must not be after 0.8.x, and a numeric component after a wildcard is rejected — numeric component after wildcard must fail. Positive (TC-104): the whole auth design leans on underscore-prefixed files are invisible to the engine, so a deliberately invalid _login.html — no doctype, no data-page, no main, not registered — must still leave check() = %d, want 0 — _login.html must stay invisible to the checker at zero.

pustaka — engine identity and site metadata (TC-089, TC-103)

Positive (TC-089): productName = %q, want pustaka and internalRoutePrefix = %q, want /__pustaka — the two constants every route and every authored page depends on. Positive (TC-103): one repository documents one product, and the header must show the site's name rather than the product's: name = %q, want Fixture Docs (product.name must not win), then version = %q, want v9.9.9; with no registry at all it falls back to the engine's own identity.

pustaka — configuration and session tokens (TC-090, TC-091, TC-092)

Negative (TC-090): every malformed environment must fail at boot rather than at the first request. Unset is unset PUSTAKA_AUTH = (%v, %v), want (nil, nil); PUSTAKA_AUTH=false must disable the feature; a missing user or password gives enabled without %s must fail fast; then a malformed TTL must fail fast, a negative TTL must fail fast, an unknown SECURE mode must fail fast and an unknown PUBLIC_HOME value must fail fast, not pick a side. The defaults are pinned too: a generated secret is 32 bytes, the TTL is 12 hours, and the docs home must be gated by default. Negative (TC-091): a fresh token verifies; an expired one must not; seven malformed shapes — empty, no dot, tampered body, tampered signature, truncated signature, oversized, trailing garbage — each give %s must not verify; and both a token signed with another secret must not verify and changing the password must invalidate existing sessions, which is what the credential fingerprint is for. Negative (TC-092): the configured pair authenticates and five near misses — wrong password, wrong user, both empty, a trailing space, an upper-cased user — give creds(%q, %q) must fail.

pustaka — what is gated, and what a denial looks like (TC-093, TC-094, TC-097, TC-105)

Negative (TC-093): sixteen next values through safeNext(%q) = %q, want %q. Same-site paths, queries and fragments survive; //evil.com, /\evil.com, an absolute URL, javascript:, a relative path, a CRLF header injection, an embedded NUL, the login and logout routes themselves, and a 600-character path all reduce to /. Negative (TC-094): twenty-nine cases decide what redirects, what 401s and what stays public — %s %s = %d, want %d. The home and index.html are gated; site.js, toc.js, the ToC parts and the vendored chart library are 401 to an XHR; the stylesheets, webfonts and the sw.js killswitch stay public; a POST is never redirected; curl gets JSON where a browser gets a 302; and neither /assets/fonts/../../guide/x.html nor /assets/../guide/x.html may ride the allowlist. Every denial also asserts a denial must not be cacheable, got %q. Negative (TC-097): an anonymous call to /__pustaka/info must not leak the size of the site — an anonymous caller must not learn the record count — while an authenticated one gets the full payload. Negative (TC-105): ordering matters, because a gzip wrapper placed outside the auth middleware frames the redirect itself: a denial must not be gzip-framed, got %q, and an allowed page should still be compressed, got %q.

pustaka — public-home mode and auth off (TC-095, TC-096, TC-098)

Positive (TC-095): PUSTAKA_AUTH_PUBLIC_HOME=1 is the escape hatch and must reproduce the allowlist exactly as it was before the home was gated — the home and all of assets/ answer 200, a guarded page and _login.html still 302, logout goes somewhere the visitor can actually see (logout Location = %q, want %q), and the back link should be visible in public-home mode: %q. Positive (TC-096): in the default gated mode the same link must be hidden — gated mode must hide the back link, got %q — and both the shipped docs/_login.html and the embedded fallback must still carry the sentinel, or %s no longer carries {{HOME_HIDDEN}} and renderLogin silently stops hiding anything. Positive (TC-098): with auth off nothing changes for the reader — with auth off, %s = %d, want 200 — the login route does not exist at all (with auth off the login route must not exist, got %d), and with auth off /info must not grow an auth key.

pustaka — the login handlers (TC-099, TC-102)

Positive (TC-099): eleven sub-cases. The page renders with no {{ left behind (placeholders were left unsubstituted) and the login page must not be cached; a signed-in visitor is forwarded instead of shown the form; form and JSON logins both succeed and the cookie carries Path=/, HttpOnly, SameSite=Lax and Max-Age=, or Set-Cookie %q is missing %q; a hostile next is reduced to /; bad credentials are a 401 carrying Incorrect username or password, and a failed login must not set a session cookie; the JSON form reports attemptsLeft; a cross-site Origin is a 403 while a same-site one is accepted; GET on logout is a 405 and logout must expire the cookie, got %q; and past the attempt limit the answer is 429 with a lockout must tell the client how long to wait. Negative (TC-102): a docs root with no _login.html must fall back to the embedded page, the on-disk one wins when present, and every placeholder is escaped — the error message must be HTML-escaped, next must not break out of its attribute, and the retry countdown should reach the page.

pustaka — the login throttle (TC-100, TC-101)

Negative (TC-100): with a stubbed clock, three failures walk the counter down — first failure left %d, want 2, then second failure left %d, want 1, then third failure = (%d, %v), want (0, 1m). After that a locked address must not be allowed, the lockout must be per address, sixty-one seconds later the lockout must expire, and reset must clear the record. Negative (TC-101): the throttle map is the one piece of per-IP state the server holds, so it is swept rather than left to grow: after a sweep interval of failures and ten minutes, sweep left %d entries, want 1 — the map must not grow without bound.

pustaka — behind a prefix strip (TC-106, TC-107, TC-108)

The docs sit behind a /docs strip, and every URL the server hands back has to carry that prefix or it lands on the landing page, which is a different service entirely. Negative (TC-106): ten header values through sitePrefix(%q) = %q, want %q — /docs and /a/b are honoured, while a protocol-relative value, a missing leading slash, a folded backslash, a CRLF injection and a 180-character path are all ignored. Positive (TC-107): trimSitePrefix(%q, %q) = %q, want %q strips only on a segment boundary, so /docsy/x.html is left alone and an already site-relative path is not stripped twice. Positive (TC-108): six behaviours behind the strip: a guarded page bounces to /docs/__pustaka/auth/login?next=%2Fguide%2Fx.html (Location = %q, want the prefixed login URL with a site-relative next); the login page's own href="/docs/assets/site.css" and form action are prefixed (login page is missing %s); a next that already carries the prefix is not doubled (the prefix was applied twice to next); the gated home (home Location = %q, want the prefixed login URL) and logout (logout Location = %q, want the prefixed login page) are prefixed too; and with no proxy in front, nothing changes — unproxied Location = %q, want the unprefixed login URL.

dashboard — the persisted task graph (TC-201…TC-206, TC-211)

T-042. M3.2 was "🟡 on the strength of its signature, not its output": PlanWorkflow was pure and produced a chain, every task carrying the previous one as its only prerequisite, and CreateWorkflow then threw the result away. A chain is acyclic the way a straight line is, which is why the cycle check had nothing to catch and DEPENDS_ON was decoration. Positive (TC-201): two picked stories become two branches off the architect rather than a queue behind each other — %s waits on %v rather than on the architect alone; two stories that wait on each other are a chain wearing a graph's name — and the security review waits on both, which is the fan-in that makes the field a slice: security waits on %v, want %v; the fan-in is per story, so its prerequisite count is a property of the record rather than a number written in the planner. Positive (TC-202): the model document's planner invariant, asserted against the sort rather than against slice order, because the old version asserted an accident of how the planner walked the stories — %s is placed at %d and its prerequisite %s at %d; DEPENDS_ON points from the dependent to the prerequisite, so the prerequisite runs first. Negative (TC-203): the check lives in CreateWorkflow, not in PlanWorkflow, because the planner cannot build a cycle and the seam is public — a plan that loops was accepted; a task graph with no first task can never be scheduled, and the scheduler would spin over it forever, it must carry its members (the refusal is %T and does not carry the cycle; sending an operator back to the wizard without naming a row is a dead end (B-3)), and it must leave nothing behind: a refused plan wrote %d task(s); every check in CreateWorkflow runs before the first write precisely so a rejection leaves nothing behind. Negative (TC-204): the other unusable plan, kept separate because the operator's next action differs — a task depending on something outside the plan was accepted; the DEPENDS_ON edge would point at a vertex that does not exist and the task would never be ready, and the refusal reads %q and does not name the missing prerequisite. Negative (TC-205): two id bugs that were cosmetic until vertices existed. PlanWorkflow numbers from t-1 every time and wf-%d was built from len(desks)+1, which never changes — two workflows left %d tasks on the desk, want %d; the second plan overwrote the first rather than joining it, %s appears twice; Task (tenantId, id) is UNIQUE, so the second write would upsert over the first and inherit its DEPENDS_ON edges, and the two workflows are called %v; len(desks)+1 does not change between creations, which is how every workflow ended up being wf-7. Positive (TC-206): the decision that the graph is read from the store and not folded, pinned so a later reader does not tidy it into the reducer — the folded floor differs between two runs whose only difference is the task graph; the reducer is reading TaskPlanned's payload, and FloorState has just grown a copy of the graph that every snapshot rung now carries, with the other half of the argument as its companion: the graph is empty after planning; not being folded is the reason it must be persisted, and a graph that is neither is not state at all. Positive (TC-211): and therefore BR-01 is proved by reload rather than by fold, which is the same way TestArcadeStoreRebuildsTheFloorFromTheGraph proves it for the floor — this one needs a reachable ArcadeDB and skips without one. the reopened graph holds %d tasks, %d were written -- the graph is not folded from the stream, so load() is the only way back and it did not bring them; the ordinal comes back because the slice order has to be a property of the data (%s came back on workflow %q at ordinal %d, was %q at %d; the ordinal is what puts the slice back in plan order, which D-1 needs to be a property of the data rather than of the bucket layout); the edge direction is asserted rather than assumed, because a reversed edge round-trips perfectly and inverts every readiness answer — %s waits on %v after the reopen, wrote %v; DEPENDS_ON points from the dependent to its prerequisite, and an edge that came back reversed would invert every readiness answer computed from it; and the workflow counter is restored from the ids in the graph, not from a count: %s was written twice after a restart; the workflow counter restarted too, and Task (tenantId, id) UNIQUE means the second write upserted over the first.

dashboard — the task status machine (TC-207, TC-208, TC-209, TC-210)

T-042, and a second state machine rather than an extension of the first. dashboard/state.go is about Desk.State — seven lowercase names, one desk per product, each of which model.go calls "a distinct CSS selector in app.css (data-state)" — while Task.status is ten uppercase names on a different cardinality, four of which (PENDING, READY, DISPATCHED, RUNNING) are never a desk's state at all. Positive (TC-207): same shape as TC-131 one table over — the table declares %s -> %s and canTaskTransition refuses it, and a status may always reach itself, because %s -> %s refused; recording a beat on a task that is already there is still a write, and every call site would need its own exception. Negative (TC-208): fourteen rows (since v0.44.0: DONE→READY left the list as the send-back row, D-084, asserted by TC-504), each carrying the reason it is illegal and each printing that reason — %s -> %s is permitted; %s — so a rule nobody can explain does not get deleted later as an oversight. An undeclared status is a typo rather than a task that cannot move, and gets its own message in both directions: "SHIPPING" is not a task status and the machine accepted it and the machine accepted a move out of a status it does not declare. Negative (TC-209): BR-34 as its own test, because DEFERRED → FAILED reads as an omission — every other non-terminal status reaches FAILED, and a reader adding the row would be making the table symmetrical and the product wrong. DEFERRED -> FAILED is permitted; BR-34 says a deferral is not a failure, and a task that failed for want of budget has spent a retry it never used, the boundary is the only way through (moveTask carried a deferred task to FAILED; the table is the boundary and a caller that can go around it means there is no boundary), the refusal is a rejection rather than a 500 (moveTask returned %v, which is not an ErrRejected; BR-06 wants a reason a handler can put in a toast, not a 500), and a refused move must not have written anyway: the task is %q after a refused move, want DEFERRED — a rejected transition that still wrote is worse than one that was never checked. Positive (TC-210): the Go vocabulary and the DDL's, compared as sets in both directions. TC-146 next door checks that one name is present, which catches a DDL missing a state Go can reach but not a Go constant the DDL would refuse — and that one fails at commit time in production and in no test at all. Task.status permits %q; Go declares %q — a status the DDL refuses is a row the scheduler cannot write, and it fails at commit rather than here, and the other way, the DDL permits %q and taskstate.go declares no transitions for it; a status nothing can produce or leave is a state the machine cannot reach.

dashboard — the scheduler, and the property it has to have (TC-212…TC-219)

T-048 and T-049. D-1 asks for something stronger than a working scheduler — "a pure function of the graph, such that two replicas computing it independently reach the same answer" — and the sentence after it is the reason: leader election then protects against duplicate dispatch rather than divergent state, which is BR-01 at the one layer that would otherwise need authoritative state of its own. Positive (TC-212): the shuffle is the test. Two replicas read the same rows out of the same database and are not promised the same order across buckets, so a scheduler that inherited slice order would pass a naive version of this and diverge only in production — two replicas over the same graph produced different decisions (%+v vs %+v); D-1 makes the scheduler a function of the graph, and a scheduler that depends on row order is a function of the database's bucket layout. Paired with the plain purity half, the same shape TestTheEgressPassIsPure uses next door: two identical calls disagreed; a decision that varies between calls becomes an event, and a replay would disagree with the run it replays. And a fixture that scheduled nothing would prove nothing, so the fixture scheduled nothing, so agreeing about it proves nothing. Negative (TC-213): purity cannot be tested by calling the function — a clock read that happened to return the same value twice passes every behavioural test there is — so this parses dashboard/scheduler.go instead, in the mould of TC-193. The import list is the test: scheduler.go imports %s; a scheduler that can reach the clock is one edit from reading it, and D-1 says the answer must be a function of the graph alone. No declaration may have a receiver (scheduler.go declares a method (%s); a method on the store has the lock and the rng in scope by definition, and the file's whole claim is that it has neither), and nothing may reach the machinery the imports keep out: scheduler.go reads .%s; SchedulerInput is the only input the decision may have, and every field on it is a value the caller already resolved. Positive (TC-214): BRD §7.4 is prescriptive about the number — all five blocking reasons "must be surfaced in the UI as the blocking reason … because an unexplained idle floor is indistinguishable from a broken product" — so the count is asserted rather than assumed: the scheduler declares %d blocking reasons, BRD §7.4 names five; a sixth is a reason the UI has no row for, and a fifth missing is a floor that looks idle for a reason nobody can explain. Each one is then produced by an input that breaks its condition, because a constant nothing emits is a row the UI renders blank forever — %s did not block the task; %s is a reason the scheduler declares and nothing produces — and budget is the one at real risk, since its guard is M10.3 and does not exist yet. Every block carries a specific: %s blocked with no detail; a reason without a specific is a dead end (B-3) and leaves the operator nothing to act on. Negative (TC-215): the rule Screen already states for the egress pass, applied here — every reason is computed before one is reported — a task failing all five reported %q; readiness comes first because below the planning floor nothing else is worth saying, and the reported reason must not depend on which check ran first, and once only: one task produced %d blocked entries; a task has one reason at a time or the floor shows the same desk five times. Negative (TC-216): CreateWorkflow refuses a cycle at planning time, so one reaching the scheduler means the graph was written by something else or edited since — %d task(s) in a three-way cycle were dispatched; none of them can ever have its prerequisite satisfied, every member is named (%d of 3 cycle members were reported blocked; a member the floor does not mention is a desk sitting idle for no stated reason), and it is not a sixth reason: %s in a cycle reported %q; the BRD makes acyclicity part of the dependency condition rather than a sixth reason. Negative (TC-217): BR-07 at the scheduler, which is not the same check as the planner-side refusal TC-156 asserts. The planner refuses to produce a plan below the floor; this refuses to dispatch a plan produced above it whose record has decayed since, which is the only way a task reaches here under a failing gate. Both boundaries: a task exactly at the floor of %d was not dispatched; BR-07 says BELOW the floor, and an off-by-one here refuses work that is allowed to run, and a task on a record below the planning floor was dispatched; BR-07 ends "Nothing is dispatched", and a model called against a record this thin is money spent on a brief nobody finished. Negative (TC-218): the within-one-pass half of D-3 — %d tasks were dispatched into one workspace; D-3 exists because "two runs in one workspace corrupt each other silently", and the seat as well as the workspace: both tasks were seated on %s; one agent cannot carry two runs, and the conflict would surface as a lease failure rather than as a decision. Since v0.25.0 the "both may run" half uses two products rather than two workspaces of one: BR-38 made the product the unit, so two workspaces of one product no longer license two runs. Positive (TC-219): the last rng on a scheduling path, removed. advance() reseated with cand[s.rng.IntN(len(cand))], which was survivable while one process owned the floor and stops being survivable at D-1 — two stores with different seeds seated different agents (%v vs %v); fixtures.go says a dispatch that seated a different agent on every replay would make the fold disagree with the run, and D-1 extends that to two replicas.

dashboard — the exclusive workspace lease (TC-220…TC-226) — retired in v0.29.0

Retired in v0.29.0 (M14.7)

The code these cases tested was deleted when GAOS stopped running a model tool itself (D-055, D-060). Every row is struck in the register with its successor — TC-295 holds one live turn per product in the graph. The prose below is kept as the record of what they asserted at v0.28.0, and nothing in the repository runs it.

T-051. D-3 asks for an exclusive workspace lease with an expiry, and names the failure it is written against: "two runs in one workspace corrupt each other silently." That failure is not loud — two agents editing one checkout do not crash, they produce a diff neither of them wrote, which is then reviewed, scored and possibly merged. The enforcement is an O_EXCL lockfile at .gaos/run/LEASE.json rather than a row, because the thing being protected is a directory and the only primitive that excludes atomically at that granularity between processes is the filesystem. Negative (TC-220): a second run took a workspace that was already leased; D-3 exists because two runs in one workspace corrupt each other silently, and neither would crash; the refusal has to be identifiable (the refusal is %v, which is not an ErrLeaseHeld; a caller cannot tell a busy workspace from a broken disk) and has to carry the holder, because the refusal is %+v and does not name the holder; the reconciler needs the pid and the expiry to decide between adopting, requeuing and orphaning. The loser must also leave the file alone: the lease now reads %+v; the refused acquire wrote over the holder it was refused by. Negative (TC-221): the row most likely to be “fixed” later, so it is asserted rather than left to a comment. An expired lease looks takeable — the expiry exists and it passed — but expiry says the supervisor stopped renewing, and D-2 detaches the child precisely so it outlives its supervisor: an expired lease was taken by a new run (err=%v); the expiry says the supervisor stopped renewing and says nothing about the child it was supervising, which D-2 detached so it would outlive exactly this. Clearing one is BreakLease, a separate name for an operation only safe once the previous run has been made dead (D-053). Positive (TC-222): a run could not re-acquire its own lease: %v; a retry after a partial write would deadlock against itself. Negative (TC-223): the renewal half, whose negative is the one that matters — a run that does not hold the lease extended it (err=%v); a supervisor declared dead and broken off could keep somebody else's workspace alive, which hands one directory to two runs through the back door. A beat that does not extend is equally wrong: the expiry is %d after a renewal, want 2300; a beat that does not extend the lease is a beat that lets the workspace expire under a live run. Negative (TC-224): a run released a lease it did not hold (err=%v), and the lockfile must actually go — the lockfile survived a release; the next run would be refused by a lease whose holder has finished. Negative (TC-225): the corrupt-file case, where the direction of the answer is the whole test. Treating an unparseable lease as “no lease” would hand the workspace to a second run on the strength of a truncated write: a truncated lease read as ok=%v err=%v; an unreadable lease must not read as an absent one, or a half-written file hands the workspace to a second run, and the safe answer must not stop at the boundary — leaseStateOf called an unreadable lease free; the scheduler would dispatch into a workspace nothing could prove was empty. Positive (TC-226): the property that makes a file the right primitive at all — a value read back by something that never held the original, which is what an adopting orchestrator is: the lease round-tripped as %+v, wrote %+v; a process that has never seen the database has only this file to work out whose workspace it is. The four intervals are asserted in the same case because the relationship between them is the design: the intervals are beat=%d stale=%d ttl=%d; a beat slower than the staleness window declares every live run dead, and a ttl inside it expires the lease before the beat is even missed, and D-053's separation of the two cadences — the Heartbeat event cadence is %ds against a %ds beat; D-053 separates them so a twenty-minute run writes five pills rather than forty into a stream that is append-only and rendered in full.

dashboard — the supervisor and its heartbeat (TC-227…TC-235)

Since v0.29.0 the supervisor runs gateway turns only, and the four cases that survived — TC-227…TC-230, in a new dashboard/runner_test.go — drive the fake ai-manager rather than a stand-in binary. What follows from here to TC-232 is the record of the detached runner. T-050 and T-052. D-2 asks that work run in detached processes that survive the orchestrator, "an orchestrator restart must not kill a paid model call in flight", and until this landed the code did the opposite on the one path an operator actually uses: Dispatch built its context from the caller's, serve() builds the caller's from signal.NotifyContext(SIGINT, SIGTERM), so a graceful shutdown cancelled the context and CommandContext killed the child. SIGKILL was already fine by accident — the Go runtime never runs and Setpgid means no terminal signal arrives — so the ungraceful path satisfied D-2 and the graceful one violated it, which is the wrong way round. Positive (TC-232): context.WithoutCancel is the fix, and this is the assertion on it — the child reported %q after its caller's context was cancelled; D-2 says work runs in detached processes that survive the orchestrator, and an orchestrator restart must not kill a paid model call in flight. The full property, killing the orchestrator process, cannot be a Go test: the test binary is the parent of everything it spawns and go test reaps the tree on exit, so the harness undoes the thing under test. That is a shell script and it belongs with T-053. Retired in v0.29.0 with the CLI execution layer (M14.7); see TC-298. Positive (TC-227): the three names, in order, around one gateway turn — the turn wrote %v, want %v; RunStarted before the work and RunExited after it is the shape a replay reads a run's duration from (SessionOpened is left out of the comparison: the session is transport, BR-41) — and the money reaching the record: the attempt's basis is %q and the desk reads %.2f; D-062 measures the gateway's cost_usd, and a cost nothing recorded is a cost the budget guard will never see. Negative (TC-228): the discipline dashboard/events.go states, checked rather than trusted. A brief that names no task is refused before anything is recorded or sent — a turn that never started wrote %v and sent %d request(s); a RunStarted for a turn nobody sent is invented evidence. Positive (TC-229): D-053 made checkable, and against the pure half so it does not take twelve minutes to run: the same twelve minutes wrote %d Heartbeat events, want 3; D-053 separates the cadences so a run does not put one pill per beat into a stream that is append-only, snapshotted every %d events and rendered in full. Both endpoints always write, because a run with no opening beat has no start for a replay to measure silence from. Positive (TC-230): BR-33 as a comparison rather than a promise. Recovery polls a request this process did not submit, so Resume is a second entry point — and a second place to drift: an adopted turn produced %+v and a supervised one %+v; BR-33 asks that nothing above the seam be able to tell them apart. The prompt is the one honest difference and is blanked before the comparison: the adopting process did not send it. And the adopted turn's basis is %q, want measured; the cost travels with the request's result precisely so this works. Negative (TC-231): handoff.go has said since it was written that the document "is declared and empty before the invocation starts", and nothing did it. A retry in a reused workspace would read the previous attempt's document — a run that wrote no handoff reported %q, want %q; the previous attempt's document was still on disk and was read as this one's, and the direction of that mistake is the worst available: the attempt's outcome is %q; a stale document made a run that produced nothing look like one that succeeded. Retired in v0.29.0 with the CLI execution layer (M14.7); see TC-281. Positive (TC-233): the lease survived a %s run (held=%v err=%v); the workspace can never be scheduled again and nothing short of a restart would notice. Retired in v0.29.0 with the CLI execution layer (M14.7); see TC-295. Negative (TC-234): a run was supervised with no workspace; there would be nothing to lease and nothing to be exclusive about. Retired in v0.29.0 with the CLI execution layer (M14.7); see TC-291. Negative (TC-235): the money guard. Five configuration guards stand in front of a paid call — GAOS_MODEL_MODE defaults to off, only serve() builds a real adapter, GAOS_WORKSPACE_ROOT is unset, GAOS_RUNNER is off — and every one can be switched on by a developer with a good reason who then runs go test ./.... So the sixth is in the code: refuseIfUnderTest permitted the real provider command from a test binary; a paid model call triggered by go test is money that does not come back, and it must not block the stand-in, because a guard that blocks them is a guard somebody will delete. The case also parses adapter_cli.go to check the guard is still on the spawn path, because every test in this package would still pass without it, because they all drive the fake, and the deletion would only surface as a bill. Retired in v0.29.0 with the CLI execution layer (M14.7); see TC-275.

dashboard — boot reconciliation (TC-236…TC-242) — retired in v0.29.0

Retired in v0.29.0 (M14.7)

The code these cases tested was deleted when GAOS stopped running a model tool itself (D-055, D-060). Every row is struck in the register with its successor — TC-297 adopts a turn by its request id and TC-298 orphans and requeues a lost one by its deadline. The prose below is kept as the record of what they asserted at v0.28.0, and nothing in the repository runs it.

T-053. D-4 does not hedge: "On boot, reconcile every task claiming to be dispatched or running against its lease and heartbeat: adopt, requeue, or fail as orphaned. This is the whole of recovery; there is no other path." Positive (TC-236): the architecture page's pseudocode, one row per branch, each carrying the reason it is what it is — a live lease with a fresh beat is adopted, because "killing or restarting it would throw away work that is going fine"; a live lease with a stale beat is requeued, because "the supervisor stopped beating, which says nothing about the child — D-2 detached it precisely so it would outlive its supervisor"; an expired lease is orphaned, because "a task that cannot be proved alive is not assumed alive". Failures read %s was %s, want %s; %s with that reason substituted, and every decision must carry one: the decision carries no reason; a recovery nobody can explain is a recovery nobody can audit. Negative (TC-237): D-4's scope is a scope rather than a filter — %d of the ten statuses were reconciled, want 2; D-4 scopes recovery to DISPATCHED and RUNNING, and a task in any other status is not claiming a process, and neither may be skipped: %s was not reconciled; a task claiming a process that nobody checked is the hole recovery exists to close. Negative (TC-238): the same structural discipline the scheduler is held to, for a sharper reason — this runs once per boot over evidence nobody can reproduce afterwards: reconcile.go imports %s; the whole of recovery has to be a function of the evidence it was handed, and a file that can reach the clock is one edit from a recovery nobody can reproduce. Positive (TC-239): the closed catalog, kept closed — both recoveries carry rule %q; they share TaskResumed, so the rule is the only thing that distinguishes them and a reader of the stream could not tell an adopted run from a requeued one. Negative (TC-240): D-054's teeth, and the case most likely to be read as over-caution. Requeue promises the previous run has been made dead; with nothing able to kill it that promise cannot be kept, and a requeue whose kill silently did nothing is the second run in one workspace wearing a reassuring event saying it was handled — a requeue with nothing able to kill emitted %s, want %s; the requeue would have handed the workspace on while claiming the previous run was dead, which is the corruption D-3 exists to prevent reached through the recovery routine. Positive (TC-241): the task is %s after adoption, want %s; nothing stopped, so nothing should have moved, and adoption broke the lease; the run is still going and its workspace is still its own. Positive (TC-242): BRD §7.5's exception row — an orphaned run kept its lease; the workspace can never be scheduled again, and the expiry that made it an orphan would go on making it one forever.

kill-mid-task.sh — build-order step 5 (TC-243…TC-246) — retired in v0.29.0

Retired in v0.29.0 (M14.7)

The code these cases tested was deleted when GAOS stopped running a model tool itself (D-055, D-060). Every row is struck in the register with its successor — TC-297 and TC-298: a turn runs upstream, so nothing on this host can die with it. The prose below is kept as the record of what they asserted at v0.28.0, and nothing in the repository runs it.

The acceptance test the whole of M4.2 exists to satisfy: "Kill the orchestrator mid-task; the work survives and is re-adopted." It cannot be a Go test, and pretending otherwise would be worse than not having it. The property is that a child outlives its parent; under go test the test binary is the parent of everything it spawns and the harness reaps the tree on exit, so the framework undoes the one thing being asserted. TC-232 gets as close as Go can by cancelling a context, which covers context.WithoutCancel and nothing beyond it. Positive (TC-243) / Negative (TC-244): the assertion the script exists for — the child outlived the orchestrator that started it, against the child died with its orchestrator — D-2 says work runs in detached processes that survive it. Positive (TC-245) / Negative (TC-246): the recovery half — boot reconciliation ran before the listener came up and the run was adopted rather than restarted or orphaned, against no adoption in the timeline — a lease held and a beat fresh is the adopt branch (D-4). It closes with step 5: the work survived and was re-adopted. Two limits, both deliberate and both printed rather than discovered. It runs against a scratch ArcadeDB and never against dev or prod, because recovery writes to the graph and those instances belong to the wider stacks. And it cannot run with GAOS_STORE=memory: MemStore loses every task record on restart, so there is no claim left to reconcile — recovery is a property of the ArcadeDB deployment, and a green run against memory would have proved nothing. The provider tool is the stand-in, with a sleep long enough to be killed around; the thing under test is the orchestrator's recovery, not a model's output.

dashboard — one record per attempt, and the slice it was given (TC-247…TC-258)

T-054, T-055 and T-056, in dashboard/taskrun_test.go, driven since v0.29.0 by the fake ai-manager (gateway_fixture_test.go) rather than a stand-in binary. The thing these guard is not a feature but a claim: D-6 says the slice sent to a model is frozen and stored per attempt, and the failure mode is not a crash — it is a route that keeps answering, plausibly, with a slice it recomputed from a record that has since moved on. Positive (TC-247): two attempts, two records — two attempts left %d record(s); BR-22 makes each attempt its own record, and a retry that overwrites the attempt it retried destroys the only evidence the retry policy has to reason from, with both attempts share the run id %q; two rows that cannot be told apart are one row beside it. Positive (TC-248): the fields are the point of the record — the record holds cost %.4f / %d in / %d out and the attempt reported %.4f / %d / %d; a figure that does not survive the write is a figure the budget guard will never see, plus the record says invocation %q, adapter mode is %q; D-13 requires it recorded precisely so the prohibition on branching can be checked rather than trusted; the log reference became the request id and the stored reply — the record names no turn or keeps no reply; BR-25 keeps the output out of the vertex and BR-41 keeps it in GAOS, so the artifact is the only way back to it. Negative (TC-249): the tempting mistake, since 04-execution.sql defaults costBasis to estimated. The gateway refuses the submit, so no turn ran — a refused submit came back as an error (%v); it is a failed attempt with a signature, and a turn the gateway refused was recorded as %+v; want one failed attempt whose cost is not measured, because nothing was, and BR-31 forbids aggregating it with figures that were. Positive (TC-250): the freeze happens on the operator's route too, with no runner behind it — nothing was frozen for digest %s; D-6 stores the slice, and a slice reassembled later answers a different question convincingly, and every citation must carry a role, because role is mandatory on USES_CONTEXT and an unlabelled edge answers only that a node was there. Negative (TC-251): the honesty of the audit edge — the cleared packet cites %d node(s), want 1; a citation whose part was redacted claims the model was given something the pass refused to send. Positive (TC-252): identity by digest — two attempts on identical context stored %d slices, want 1; storing it twice makes comparing two runs a diff of two copies of one string, which is what H-4's comparison scope will read. Positive (TC-253): the route itself — an attempt has run and the route still computes a slice live; GET /tasks/{id}/context has to match what the model actually received (D-6), and before anything runs the opposite: a desk that has never run an attempt reported a frozen slice; there is no evidence to serve and saying otherwise is the failure D-6 describes. Negative (TC-254): the case that says why freezing is worth the storage — the frozen slice's digest moved from %q to %q when the record was edited; a slice that follows the record is not evidence of anything. Positive (TC-255): recovery closes what it gave up on — a request the gateway answers 404 for, past its deadline — the recovered attempt is still %q, want %q; a run the reconciler gave up on and left marked RUNNING is the graph asserting a process nobody believes in. Positive (TC-256, TC-257): the ArcadeDB half, which cannot be written against the interface for the same reason TC-126 cannot — restart the process is not something Store offers. the reopened store answers with a live assembly; the slice was written and load() did not bring it back, so the process that could answer what digest %s contained is gone, and for the attempt, the reopened store holds %d attempt(s), one was written; a TaskRun that does not survive a restart is an attempt the retry guard will never count. Both skip without GAOS_ARCADE_TEST_URL; see scratch.sh. Negative (TC-258): the replay handle reads and still refuses to write — the read-only handle refused TaskContext: %v; a handle that cannot read is not a replay handle.

One finding, in the same class as D-047

TC-256 and the conformance suite failed on the first ArcadeDB run with "Upsert must involve an index to retrieve the records" — ContextSlice is now written by business id and had no (tenantId, id) index. That is precisely the case D-047 predicted when it said "the next task that writes one of them adds its index in the same change", so the index was added under the same heading and no new ruling was needed. The DDL applies 324 statements rather than 323; dashboard/schema/RECONCILIATION.md records it.

dashboard — the reply contract, and the handoff kept for the next agent (TC-259…TC-267)

Since v0.29.0 the five cases that survived live in dashboard/contract_test.go and test the reply contract of D-057; the packet on disk (TC-262…TC-265) went with the CLI adapter. T-061…T-064, originally in dashboard/packet_test.go. Until these existed a real provider tool could be spawned and could not possibly succeed: it was handed a working directory with nothing in it and an instruction that never said what to produce, so it would do the work, exit 0, and fail the attempt with handoff-missing every single time. Positive (TC-259): the case that would have caught the whole feature being useless — the contract's own example does not parse: %v; a model that copies it faithfully fails the attempt, and the contract is the only instruction it got. The contract and the parser are two files written at different times, and nothing else makes them agree. Positive (TC-260): the contract never mentions %q; a model cannot satisfy a requirement nobody stated. Negative (TC-264): path %q resolved to %q instead of being refused; the workspace is a lease and anything outside it belongs to somebody else. Asserted at insideWorkspace rather than through MaterialisePacket, because driving it from the top would mean forging a Cleared and TC-175 walks the syntax tree to forbid that — a guard that caught this test being written the easy way. Retired in v0.29.0 with the CLI execution layer (M14.7); see TC-286. Negative (TC-265): a run that wrote no handoff came back with failure %q, want %q; BR-30 makes the document mandatory and the exit code says only that the process ran. Retired in v0.29.0 with the CLI execution layer (M14.7); see TC-281. Positive (TC-266): the turn replied with a handoff and the store holds none for the next attempt; the session is transport, the artifact is the record, and the next packet does not cite %s; a document the next agent cannot be shown to have received is not a handoff. Porting it found a bug: since v0.27.0 the prompt and the reply are stored as artifacts beside the handoff, and the lookup took the newest artifact of any kind, so the next agent was cited the previous turn's reply as its lesson. It now takes handoffs only. Negative (TC-267): attempt 1 of t-1 was cited artifact %s, which attempt 1 of t-1 produced; an attempt reading its own output is a loop with extra steps.

live-cli-run.sh — the first real model call (TC-268…TC-273) — retired in v0.29.0

Retired in v0.29.0 (M14.7)

The code these cases tested was deleted when GAOS stopped running a model tool itself (D-055, D-060). Every row is struck in the register with its successor — TC-300…TC-303, live-aim-run.sh. The prose below is kept as the record of what they asserted at v0.28.0, and nothing in the repository runs it.

The only script in this repository that spends money, and the only check anywhere that can answer the question the CLI approach rests on: does a real provider tool, handed GAOS's packet and nothing else, produce a document GAOS can read? A fake tool told to write a document writes it whatever the packet said, so every other check here proves the orchestrator and not the contract.

Opt-in twice over: GAOS_LIVE_FAKE=1 exercises the whole harness for nothing, and GAOS_LIVE_OK=1 is the real call. With neither it prints what it would do and stops. The agent is given a git worktree at a temporary path, detached at HEAD, never the working tree; nothing is committed and nothing is pushed. Positive (TC-268): ok claude present — 2.1.241 (Claude Code) and ok dashboard ready — the boot smoke test passed against the pin, against FAIL the dashboard never became ready. Positive (TC-269): ok readiness 69 — the planning floor is 45 (BR-07), against FAIL the planner refused the workflow (HTTP %s), which on a thin record is the correct answer. Negative (TC-270): FAIL the egress pass refused the dispatch. It reads the stream rather than the status code, because a refusal is a recorded state change with a toast and a 200 — see TC-195, which pins that on purpose. Positive (TC-271): ok packet: .gaos/packet/handoff.md. Positive (TC-272): ok GAOS read the document: %s, against FAIL GAOS recorded a failed attempt: %s. Read from RunExited's text and not from a payload: Events() strips payloads at the seam, and an earlier version of this script grepped for the failure signature there and passed on every run including the one where no document was written. Negative (TC-273): FAIL %s was not created, and the weaker half, FAIL the file exists but never mentions "%s"; the packet may not have reached the model.

dashboard — what the schema apply did (TC-274)

ApplySchema has always returned two counts so the boot could say what happened, and NewArcadeStoreFor discarded both — so after a deploy that added an index, the only way to confirm it had landed was to query the database by hand. The store keeps them now and serve() prints one line: schema: 324 statements from the embedded DDL, none failed (128 accepted, 196 already declared) — 365 statements since v0.24.0 added 11-blueprint.sql. Positive (TC-274): the counts are captured — the store reports %d applied and %d present; the DDL is over three hundred statements, so both being zero means the counts were thrown away rather than that nothing ran — and the total is stable across two applies, because two applies of one embedded DDL counted %d statements then %d; the DDL is compiled into the binary and cannot change between them. Skips without GAOS_ARCADE_TEST_URL; see scratch.sh.

What the counts do not mean, measured

This case was first written to assert that a second apply against a populated database applies nothing. The database said 128. On 26.7.2 most of the DDL is IF NOT EXISTS and the server honours that silently rather than raising AlreadyExists, so applied means "the server accepted it" and never "something new was created". The pair tells an operator how large the embedded DDL is and that none of it failed; it cannot say whether anything changed. The doc comment on ApplySchema promised more than that and has been corrected, and the log line is worded accepted rather than applied for the same reason — a line implying a migration had run would send somebody looking for one that never happened.

What the first live runs measured, including two things nobody had asked

The loop works, three times. On 2026-08-23 three real Claude Code runs, spawned by the scheduler from a planned graph, were each given the packet, wrote the handoff document, and had it parsed to outcome complete. Cost is measured from the tool's own JSON envelope rather than estimated (D-051):

RunCostTurnsDurationDeliverable
1$1.283418195 s376-line runbook
2$1.079816143 s227-line runbook
3$1.183417164 s314-line runbook

Identical input, different output, and that is the useful half. Runs 2 and 3 were given byte-identical packets — same four files, same digest 1d6ffc90a0067d2f, which is why both wrote their log to a file of that name — and produced runbooks of 227 and 314 lines. GAOS's half of the system is reproducible and the model's half is not, which is exactly the split the design assumes: replay reconstructs what an attempt was given (D-6) and never what it answered, and H-4's fourth scope exists to compare two runs precisely because they differ. A frozen slice would be worth much less if they did not.

The contract has to be a standing instruction. The first attempt did the work well and never wrote the document: the contract had been the last section of a long input and read as background by the time the work was done. It is now repeated in the task's definition of done and passed to the tool as a system prompt. That is a measurement, not a preference, and dashboard/adapter_cli.go records it where the flag is set.

An agent reads more than the packet. The runbook it wrote quotes dashboard/egress.go — a file the allowlist had deliberately kept OUT of the packet. The agent read it off the worktree. The allowlist bounds what GAOS sends; in CLI mode it does not bound what the tool can open, because the tool has a working directory. That materially qualifies L-4 and it is now OQ-24.

check-workspace.sh — the agent workspace's own invariants (TC-357, TC-358, TC-380, TC-381)

A running stack hides the drift in the agent workspace: an orphan skill that contradicts the agent that owns its job, a vendored skill nothing referenced, and four numbers that stopped matching their own registers. deploy/check-stacks.sh checks the stack and pustaka check checks the docs site's structure; neither looked at .claude/. This script does, in seven checks: every path named in .claude/CLAUDE.md exists; every skill is referenced; every count agrees with its register; no rule sentence appears in two rule files; no isolation canary was left behind; and the review report store carries no secret-shaped string. The sixth check also owns the skills: no framework vocabulary came back into one, every vendored skill kept its licence and its provenance, and the trimmed design-engineering skill still matches the SHA-256 its SOURCE.md records — it is an instruction file every frontend session loads, so an edit to it fails the run until the digest is updated in the same change. It runs on the host, where deploy/rebuild-restart.sh symlinks the host-side credentials into worktrees, so for a path matching *.env, *.aim-*, *.api-*, *.pem or *.key it prints the path and the rule and never the matched line. Positive (TC-357, TC-380): the files as committed print ok on all six checks and the run exits 0. Negative (TC-358, TC-381): a stale path, a drifted count, a skill carrying framework vocabulary, a vendored skill without its licence or its provenance, or a trimmed skill whose digest moved prints FAIL <what> and the run exits 1. Since v0.45.0 the README's figures are compared with what the script computes, not with fixed strings: ok README Go-test count matches (<n> Go tests), ok README case count matches (<n> registered test cases) and ok README version matches (Product version <semver>), or FAIL README Go-test count: README.md does not say "<n> Go tests" and the same shape for the other two. It is advisory — there is no CI, so the product owner runs it (D-025).

check-workspace.sh — no private instance in a tracked file (TC-510)

Section 7, since v0.45.0 (D-085). GAOS is open source, and one operator's instance — a sample product's name, its organisation, its domain — must not be named in the repository. The patterns are not in the script: they are read from the first non-empty list of $GAOS_FORBIDDEN_STRINGS, deploy/local/forbidden-strings and the same path in the main checkout (an agent worktree never receives ignored files), one extended regex per line, case-insensitive. git grep --untracked scans the tracked and untracked-not-ignored files; the path check covers the same set. A path that matches is printed by its index and a hash, never by name, and the matched text is never printed. Positive: ok no tracked file or path names a private instance (<N> pattern(s) from local|main|env). Negative: FAIL a tracked file names a private instance: <path> (line <n>), FAIL a tracked path names a private instance: path #<k> (<8 hex>), FAIL a file under deploy/local/ is tracked: <path>, FAIL the forbidden-strings list is tracked; it must stay git-ignored, FAIL the forbidden-strings list holds an invalid pattern, FAIL GAOS_FORBIDDEN_STRINGS names no readable, non-empty list and FAIL git grep could not scan the working tree. Without a list it prints skip private strings: no forbidden-strings list (deploy/local/README.md says where it goes) and does not fail. Every branch was run once at v0.45.0 and its output piped back through the list: nothing matched.

dashboard — the design gate (TC-359…TC-375)

T-184…T-188, in dashboard/designgate_test.go. Since v0.35.0 a plan can carry a review task, and the reviewer's prompt holds the design and the rubric and nothing else — no brief, no goal, no handoff, no failure lesson, no operator's answer, no stories, no acceptance criteria. dashboard — the board scrolls (TC-412)

TC-412, negative (TestTheBoardHostHasABoundedHeight): the vendored board is height:100%; overflow:hidden with .sfb-body as its one scroller and overscroll-behavior:contain on it. In the back office's normal-flow block the host had no height, so the board grew to its content (13,000px for forty story lanes), .sfb-body could never scroll vertically, its horizontal scrollbar sat off screen, and contain stopped the wheel reaching the page: scrolling down over the board did nothing and nothing errored. The test reads static/app.css and fails with .sisflow-board-host has no bounded height (height:calc(100vh - Npx) with a min-height): the board grows to its content and .sfb-body can never scroll vertically when the host rule is gone, and with .sfb-body still has overscroll-behavior:contain: a wheel over the board never reaches the page when the second rule is. The behaviour itself was verified in a real browser (Chromium via Playwright: wheel down, wheel sideways and back up over the board at 1440×900, 1280×720 and 390×800), which this register does not run.

check-stacks.sh — the landing page (TC-622, TC-623)

Both can go stale without anything failing: a string added in one language only, or a figure typed by hand and never recounted. The page said 71 graph types and 49 event types long after both had moved. These two cases are the recount.

TC-622, positive (deploy/check-stacks.sh): ok every translatable string on the landing page has an Indonesian entry, ok the landing page's Indonesian dictionary has no unused entry, ok the landing page's Indonesian dictionary declares no key twice and ok the landing page declares at least 100 translatable keys; negative: each of the first three prints FAIL with the same message and lists the keys beneath it, when a data-i18n key in landing-page/index.html has no line in landing-page/js/i18n.js, when the dictionary keeps a key the page no longer uses, or when a key is declared twice; the fourth prints FAIL the landing page declares n translatable keys, want at least 100, which is what a broken extraction looks like.

TC-623, positive (deploy/check-stacks.sh): ok the landing page says 31 vertex types, and the schema declares 31, ok the landing page says 39 edge types, and the schema declares 39, ok the landing page says 3 document types, and the schema declares 3, ok the landing page says 73 graph types, and the schema declares 73 and ok the landing page says 59 event types, and the catalog declares 59 — the numbers are the ones true at v0.51.0, and the script prints whatever it counts; negative: a figure that disagrees prints, for example, FAIL the landing page says 40 edge types, and the schema declares 39, and a figure missing from the page prints FAIL the landing page says nothing for edge types, and the schema declares 39.

Test scenarios — not yet implemented

BRD §4.1 names three tests as the only honest proof the system works. Two are still forward specifications — Given / When / Then, so the target exists in writing before the code that would satisfy it — and neither can run today. Domain E (review, scoring & retry) is entirely Designed, not Built, per BRD §2.1; C (planning) and D (execution) have each had one requirement close since — the task graph is persisted (C-2, partly) and the scheduler is a pure function of it (D-1) — but nothing dispatches from either yet, and an acceptance test needs the whole loop rather than the first link of it.

Since 2026-11-02 this section also holds the thirty-six forward specifications for M14, below; they are ordinary register rows rather than BRD acceptance tests, and each section moves up when its code lands.

The third has moved. The fold test ran for the first time on 2026-08-17 and now lives with the implemented scenarios, which is what closing M0.3 and M8.1 was for.

The gateway forward specifications (all running as of v0.30.0)

Thirty-six cases were written on 2026-11-02, before any of the code they test, for M14: the move to ai-manager as the one gateway, the Blueprint, and a board per product (D-055…D-062). There is no script or test to quote yet, so what is fixed here is the asserted value — the failure signature, the event, the status code — in code. Each h3 moves to the implemented scenarios in the commit that makes its cases run, and from then on quotes the test's exact t.Fatalf or the script's exact ok/FAIL line, like every other section. The Go tests run against an httptest fake ai-manager that scripts a reply per request id and counts the POSTs it receives; none spawns a binary and none spends money. Only live-aim-run.sh calls a real model. TC-275…TC-290 run as of v0.24.0, TC-291…TC-295 as of v0.25.0, TC-296…TC-303 as of v0.27.0, TC-304…TC-307 and TC-309 as of v0.28.0, and TC-308 and TC-310 as of v0.30.0, all moved up to the implemented scenarios (the task fold, the vendored board). None of the thirty-six is still waiting.

The unsatisfiable task (TC-048, TC-049)

Given a task whose acceptance criterion contradicts a domain rule (the shape BR-07 and BR-14 describe), when it is dispatched through the scheduler and runner, then (TC-048, positive) the system stops within the six guard limits (BR-17: 3 retries, 2 escalations, 2 identical failure signatures, budget cap) and leaves a human-queue item carrying a recorded reason code (BR-18). TC-049, negative: if it instead loops past the guard limits, or stops without a reason code, the test has failed — in the design's own words, "looping or failing silently means the retry loop is not finished." Blocked by: domains C, D, E — none built.

The second run, a week apart (TC-052, TC-053)

Given the same objective run twice on the same product a week apart, when the second run completes, then (TC-052, positive) it needs fewer retries and less human intervention than the first (§1.4). TC-053, negative: if it needs the same or more, then "the extraction step is decorative and everything above it is expensive theatre" — a product-viability signal that should trigger review of domain G (memory & learning), not an automatic block. Blocked by: the entire control loop, domains C–H, and by the fact that this run is also how the numeric baseline gets set for the first time — the one thing D-039 defers until it exists.
